For supply-chain managers, planners, buyers, logistics coordinators, warehouse leaders, procurement teams, customs staff, and operations managers.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Explain signed forecast error, distinguish bias from error size, and keep an unconfirmed promotional uplift separate from the baseline.
  • Request a usable supplier commitment by separating acknowledgment, quantities, shipment timing, arrival timing, and unresolved dependencies.
  • Explain item-level availability and service measures without treating a high total balance or safety-stock policy as proof of fulfillment.
  • Reconcile a changing order queue, distinguish gross completions from net reduction, and avoid unsupported claims about order age or service.
  • Give a useful delay update while separating a carrier estimate, confirmed transport events, appointment timing, and an authorized contingency.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

01Demand Planning and Forecast BiasThree optimistic forecasts and a new promotion+

Your goal

Explain signed forecast error, distinguish bias from error size, and keep an unconfirmed promotional uplift separate from the baseline.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Three optimistic forecasts and a new promotion

Demand planner Aisha and sales lead Martin review monthly forecasts of 120, 130, and 140 units against verified demand of 100, 110, and 120. For this case, signed error means forecast minus actual demand. Next month's baseline is 120 units. Sales proposes a 30-unit promotional uplift but has no order evidence for it. Aisha and Martin must describe the three-month pattern and preserve the promotion as a scenario without treating either historical bias or future uplift as a certainty forever.

Aisha: Demand planner / Martin: Sales lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is each month's signed error under the stated convention?

Answer and explanation

A. Plus 20 units

Each forecast exceeds its corresponding actual demand by twenty units.

2. What is the next-month baseline?

Answer and explanation

D. 120 units

The brief states a baseline of 120 and a separate proposed uplift of thirty.

3. What supports the promotional uplift as confirmed orders?

Answer and explanation

D. No order evidence is supplied.

The promotion is a proposed scenario, and the case explicitly supplies no supporting order evidence.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

With error defined as forecast minus actual, the three errors are +20, +20, and +20 units. Total error is +60 and mean error is +20. Positive means overforecasting under this convention; other systems may reverse the sign.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
demand forecast
An estimate of future demand for a defined item and period.prepare a demand forecast
actual demand
The observed demand under the stated measurement definition.reconcile actual demand
forecast bias
A tendency for forecasts to be systematically too high or too low.monitor forecast bias
signed error
The forecast difference calculated with an explicitly stated direction.state the signed-error convention
mean error
The average signed forecast error across the defined observations.calculate mean error
absolute error
The size of a forecast error without its positive or negative sign.compare absolute errors
mean absolute error
The average absolute error across the defined observations.report mean absolute error
overforecast
A forecast greater than the corresponding actual demand.identify an overforecast
underforecast
A forecast below the corresponding actual demand.investigate an underforecast
baseline forecast
The reference forecast before a separately identified adjustment.preserve the baseline forecast
promotional uplift
Additional demand expected because of a promotion.qualify promotional uplift
incremental demand
Demand additional to the specified base rather than displaced from elsewhere.estimate incremental demand
cannibalization
A gain in one product's demand at the expense of another's.assess demand cannibalization
seasonality
A recurring demand pattern tied to a calendar or cycle.account for seasonality
trend
A sustained directional movement in a series over time.distinguish trend from noise
forecast horizon
The future period covered by a forecast.define the forecast horizon
forecast lag
The time between making a forecast and the period being forecast.compare forecasts at the same lag
forecast snapshot
A saved version of the forecast at a specified time.preserve the forecast snapshot
consensus forecast
A forecast agreed through the relevant cross-functional planning process.review the consensus forecast
demand signal
Information indicating demand behavior or potential change.evaluate a demand signal
booked order
An order recorded under the organization's order definition.distinguish booked orders
scenario assumption
A premise used to construct a possible future case.document the scenario assumption
forecast override
A deliberate adjustment to a model or baseline forecast.justify a forecast override
forecast value added
Improvement in a defined forecast measure from a specified planning step.evaluate forecast value added

Zero mean signed error does not prove perfect forecasts: +10 and -10 cancel even though both forecasts missed. Mean absolute error would be 10. Next month, 120 plus a proposed 30 is a 150-unit scenario, not 150 confirmed orders.

C Notice the language

Separate optimism from evidence

A sales opportunity can deserve attention without being entered as confirmed demand. Acknowledge the opportunity, then ask for the assumption and evidence. Keeping baseline and upside visible lets colleagues discuss the commercial case without rewriting historical results or pretending that a forecast is an order.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the convention
Here we define signed error as forecast minus actual demand.
Describe the pattern
Each of the three forecasts exceeded actual demand by 20 units.
Name the average
Mean signed error is plus 20 units over these three months.
Limit the conclusion
This pattern does not prove every future forecast will be high.
Separate size and direction
Absolute error measures the miss without its sign.
Keep the reference
The next-month baseline remains 120 units.
Identify the increment
Sales proposes a 30-unit promotional uplift.
Qualify the scenario
That produces a 150-unit scenario, not confirmed demand.
Ask for evidence
What order or response evidence supports the proposed increment?
Avoid historical erasure
A future promotion does not change the earlier forecast errors.
Use a consistent snapshot
Compare the forecast version that existed at the agreed planning point.
Distinguish a target
A sales target is not automatically an unbiased demand estimate.
Check displacement
Is the uplift additional demand or partly shifted from another item?
Record the override
Keep the adjustment, owner, and rationale visible.
Evaluate the contribution
Assess whether the adjustment improves the defined forecast measure.
Close the discussion
Present baseline, upside, and supporting evidence separately.
6 language and precision notes
Bias
Directional tendency, not a moral judgment about the planner.
Positive
Has meaning only after the error-sign convention is stated.
Actual
Needs a measurement definition; shipments and unconstrained demand may differ.
Uplift
An increment against a specified baseline, not necessarily booked orders.
Consensus
An agreed forecast can still be wrong.
Snapshot
Preserves what was forecast at the time, avoiding hindsight substitution.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. A colleague says the promotion cancels the historical overforecast. Which correction is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. The proposed 30-unit increment does not change the three recorded errors of +20.

Historical errors concern saved forecasts and actual demand for prior periods; a new proposal does not revise them.

2. Two other errors are +10 and -10. What follows?

Answer and explanation

B. Mean error is zero, but mean absolute error is 10.

Signed errors cancel, whereas absolute errors retain the magnitude of each miss.

3. Which next-month statement is precise?

Answer and explanation

A. Baseline 120; proposed uplift 30; upside scenario 150; no order evidence supplied.

The statement separates the reference forecast, proposed increment, scenario total, and missing order evidence.

4. Why retain the original forecast snapshot?

Answer and explanation

D. To evaluate the forecast actually available at the agreed planning point

A fixed historical version prevents later knowledge from changing the forecast being evaluated.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Three optimistic forecasts and a new promotion

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Aisha: Demand planner / Martin: Sales lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • mean absolute error
  • promotional uplift
  • mean error
  • forecast override
  • booked order
  • baseline forecast
  • signed error
  • forecast snapshot
  • overforecast
  • cannibalization
  1. Martin
    Sales expects the promotion to improve demand next month. Before we add the uplift, can we agree what the last three months actually tell us?
  2. Aisha
    They show an 1 of twenty units in each month. The forecasts were one hundred twenty, one hundred thirty, and one hundred forty against lower actual demand.
  3. Martin
    The dashboard uses a positive error number. I want to make sure everyone reads the direction correctly, because another report seems to use the opposite sign.
  4. Aisha
    State the 2 convention beside it: forecast minus actual. Under this convention, each error is plus twenty; a different system may reverse that sign.
  5. Martin
    Let me check: plus sixty units in total, but plus twenty per month on average. Which figure belongs on the monthly performance slide?
  6. Aisha
    Correct. The 3 is plus twenty units across these three observations. That describes this period without proving that every future forecast must be too high.
  7. Martin
    What if positive and negative errors cancel in a later period? A zero average could look excellent even when individual months still miss by a meaningful amount.
  8. Aisha
    Compare 4 as well. If the errors are plus ten and minus ten, the signed mean is zero but the average absolute miss is ten.
  9. Martin
    For next month, the reference forecast is one hundred twenty. Sales proposes another thirty because of the promotion, but we have no supporting orders yet.
  10. Aisha
    Keep the 5 visible. We can show the thirty-unit proposal separately so people understand what comes from the reference and what comes from the new commercial assumption.
  11. Martin
    That gives a scenario of one hundred fifty. I do not want the planning team to dismiss the opportunity, but we should avoid calling it confirmed demand.
  12. Aisha
    Exactly. The 6 is an expectation to evaluate. Ask what evidence supports the increment and whether the demand is genuinely additional rather than shifted from another product.
  13. Martin
    The campaign could move some existing customers from a related item. If we count all those purchases as new demand, the aggregate plan could be overstated.
  14. Aisha
    That would require assessing 7. We should distinguish additional demand from redistribution before treating the whole increment as growth in the overall demand plan.
  15. Martin
    Sales has a target, but nobody has placed an order for that extra quantity. I will keep those two fields separate in the planning record.
  16. Aisha
    Right. A 8 has a different status from a forecast or target. We currently have no order evidence for the proposed thirty-unit increase.
  17. Martin
    When the next results arrive, let's evaluate the forecast version we actually used. Revising old numbers after seeing demand would make the performance comparison misleading.
  18. Aisha
    Preserve the 9 and its planning date. We need a consistent lag and definition if we want to learn whether the adjustment helped.
  19. Martin
    I will present one hundred twenty as baseline and one hundred fifty as the promotion scenario, with the evidence gap stated. The historical errors will remain unchanged.
  20. Aisha
    Good. Record the proposed 10 with its owner and rationale. That gives the opportunity a fair assessment without disguising uncertainty or treating the scenario as a customer commitment.

Answers and explanations
  1. overforecast Turn 2. Each forecast is twenty units above its corresponding actual demand, so each is an overforecast.
  2. signed error Turn 4. The signed-error convention here is forecast minus actual, making overforecasts positive.
  3. mean error Turn 6. Mean error averages the three signed differences: plus sixty divided by three gives plus twenty units.
  4. mean absolute error Turn 8. Mean absolute error preserves error magnitude, so opposite signed misses do not cancel each other.
  5. baseline forecast Turn 10. The baseline forecast remains the 120-unit reference against which the proposed promotional adjustment is shown.
  6. promotional uplift Turn 12. The promotional uplift is the proposed thirty-unit increment, not an already confirmed order quantity.
  7. cannibalization Turn 14. Cannibalization concerns demand transferred from another product, which may not increase total demand by the full uplift.
  8. booked order Turn 16. A booked order is recorded order demand, unlike the unsupported promotional expectation in this case.
  9. forecast snapshot Turn 18. The forecast snapshot records the version available at the agreed planning point so later information cannot rewrite its score.
  10. forecast override Turn 20. The override is the deliberate adjustment to the baseline, whose rationale and later contribution should remain visible.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Martin: Sales expects the promotion to improve demand next month. Before we add the uplift, can we agree what the last three months actually tell us?
  2. Aisha: They show an overforecast of twenty units in each month. The forecasts were one hundred twenty, one hundred thirty, and one hundred forty against lower actual demand.
  3. Martin: The dashboard uses a positive error number. I want to make sure everyone reads the direction correctly, because another report seems to use the opposite sign.
  4. Aisha: State the signed error convention beside it: forecast minus actual. Under this convention, each error is plus twenty; a different system may reverse that sign.
  5. Martin: Let me check: plus sixty units in total, but plus twenty per month on average. Which figure belongs on the monthly performance slide?
  6. Aisha: Correct. The mean error is plus twenty units across these three observations. That describes this period without proving that every future forecast must be too high.
  7. Martin: What if positive and negative errors cancel in a later period? A zero average could look excellent even when individual months still miss by a meaningful amount.
  8. Aisha: Compare mean absolute error as well. If the errors are plus ten and minus ten, the signed mean is zero but the average absolute miss is ten.
  9. Martin: For next month, the reference forecast is one hundred twenty. Sales proposes another thirty because of the promotion, but we have no supporting orders yet.
  10. Aisha: Keep the baseline forecast visible. We can show the thirty-unit proposal separately so people understand what comes from the reference and what comes from the new commercial assumption.
  11. Martin: That gives a scenario of one hundred fifty. I do not want the planning team to dismiss the opportunity, but we should avoid calling it confirmed demand.
  12. Aisha: Exactly. The promotional uplift is an expectation to evaluate. Ask what evidence supports the increment and whether the demand is genuinely additional rather than shifted from another product.
  13. Martin: The campaign could move some existing customers from a related item. If we count all those purchases as new demand, the aggregate plan could be overstated.
  14. Aisha: That would require assessing cannibalization. We should distinguish additional demand from redistribution before treating the whole increment as growth in the overall demand plan.
  15. Martin: Sales has a target, but nobody has placed an order for that extra quantity. I will keep those two fields separate in the planning record.
  16. Aisha: Right. A booked order has a different status from a forecast or target. We currently have no order evidence for the proposed thirty-unit increase.
  17. Martin: When the next results arrive, let's evaluate the forecast version we actually used. Revising old numbers after seeing demand would make the performance comparison misleading.
  18. Aisha: Preserve the forecast snapshot and its planning date. We need a consistent lag and definition if we want to learn whether the adjustment helped.
  19. Martin: I will present one hundred twenty as baseline and one hundred fifty as the promotion scenario, with the evidence gap stated. The historical errors will remain unchanged.
  20. Aisha: Good. Record the proposed forecast override with its owner and rationale. That gives the opportunity a fair assessment without disguising uncertainty or treating the scenario as a customer commitment.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Three months of overforecast before a promotion

Fictional demand review: the forecast exceeded actual demand for three months; sales expects a promotion to help, but no supporting orders exist yet.

  1. Demand planner: Our forecast exceeded actual demand for three months. That repeated direction suggests an overforecast pattern to review.
  2. Sales manager: The promotion should improve demand. Can we treat the current forecast as confirmed now?
  3. Demand planner: Not yet. We have an expectation about the promotion, but no supporting order evidence.
  4. Sales manager: Does the recent forecast bias prove that the promotion will fail as well?
  5. Demand planner: No. Historical overforecast and the future promotion are different questions, both requiring clear assumptions.
  6. Sales manager: Then I'll describe the promotion uplift as a planning assumption rather than a confirmed demand signal.
  7. Demand planner: Good. We can take a base view and a clearly labeled promotion scenario into S&OP.
  8. Sales manager: What evidence would let us revisit that assumption during the next review?
  9. Demand planner: Verified orders and relevant demand information, with timing and scope clear rather than an optimistic headline.
  10. Sales manager: I'll bring those when available and keep the current expectation separate from actual demand.
A one-time order inside the baseline

Separate fictional planning case: last month's demand included a one-time order of 200 units; whether it will repeat is unconfirmed.

  1. Sales analyst: Last month's actual demand includes the two hundred-unit special order. Should it remain in next month's baseline?
  2. Demand planner: We should identify it separately because repeat demand hasn't been confirmed for that order.
  3. Sales analyst: Could we assume it repeats simply because the customer was pleased with delivery?
  4. Demand planner: Customer satisfaction isn't a confirmed order. Keep any repeat expectation labeled as an assumption.
  5. Sales analyst: Would removing it completely prove that the lower forecast is more accurate?
  6. Demand planner: No. We need a justified planning treatment, not certainty about an outcome that hasn't occurred.
  7. Sales analyst: I'll show the one-time quantity separately and ask sales whether any repeat commitment exists.
  8. Demand planner: That gives S&OP a clearer demand signal and avoids hiding a special event in the baseline.
  9. Sales analyst: I'll distinguish confirmed history from the proposed future treatment in the review.
  10. Demand planner: Good. We'll revisit the assumption when evidence arrives instead of treating one month as a permanent pattern.
Orders shifted between months are not necessarily growth

Separate fictional demand analysis: 80 units originally requested for June were moved to May; no additional quantity was ordered.

  1. Commercial lead: May's orders increased by eighty units. Can we report that as new demand growth?
  2. Demand analyst: Those eighty were moved from June into May; the total ordered quantity didn't increase.
  3. Commercial lead: So we have a timing change in the demand signal rather than eighty incremental units?
  4. Demand analyst: Correct. Treating them as new demand could also leave the June forecast overstated.
  5. Commercial lead: Does the move prove the customer will buy less over the whole quarter?
  6. Demand analyst: No. The supplied change is timing only, not evidence of a different quarterly total.
  7. Commercial lead: I'll explain the pull-forward and keep the two months linked in the forecast discussion.
  8. Demand analyst: Please preserve the original and revised dates so we can trace the change.
  9. Commercial lead: Should we take the timing assumption into S&OP rather than celebrate it as a sales increase?
  10. Demand analyst: Yes. Report the confirmed schedule movement and leave any wider growth interpretation dependent on additional evidence.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Three optimistic forecasts and a new promotion
  1. Read the corrected forecast dialogue in pairs. Stress plus twenty, mean, and absolute when comparing the historical errors.
  2. Switch roles. Read turns 9-20, distinguishing the 120-unit baseline, proposed 30-unit uplift, and 150-unit scenario.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

The sign does not measure certainty

A forecast is 80 units and actual demand is 100. Error is defined as forecast minus actual. Next month has a 90-unit baseline and a proposed 15-unit uplift without orders.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Planner: "The signed error is ___ twenty units."

Answer and explanation

B. minus

Eighty minus one hundred equals negative twenty under the stated convention.

2. Sales: "That is an ___."

Answer and explanation

D. underforecast

Forecast demand is below actual demand in the supplied period.

3. Planner: "The next-month scenario totals one hundred ___ units."

Answer and explanation

C. five

The baseline of ninety plus fifteen gives a scenario total of 105.

4. Sales: "The uplift remains an ___, not booked orders."

Answer and explanation

A. assumption

The proposed increment has no supporting order evidence in this case.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Demand Planning and Forecast Bias
Original fictional case: Demand planner Aisha and sales lead Martin review monthly forecasts of 120, 130, and 140 units against verified demand of 100, 110, and 120. For this case, signed error means forecast minus actual demand. Next month's baseline is 120 units. Sales proposes a 30-unit promotional uplift but has no order evidence for it. Aisha and Martin must describe the three-month pattern and preserve the promotion as a scenario without treating either historical bias or future uplift as a certainty forever.
Language workshop: Three optimistic forecasts and a new promotion
Communication goal: Explain signed forecast error, distinguish bias from error size, and keep an unconfirmed promotional uplift separate from the baseline.
Language guidance: With error defined as forecast minus actual, the three errors are +20, +20, and +20 units. Total error is +60 and mean error is +20. Positive means overforecasting under this convention; other systems may reverse the sign.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Here we define signed error as forecast minus actual demand. / Each of the three forecasts exceeded actual demand by 20 units. / Mean signed error is plus 20 units over these three months. / This pattern does not prove every future forecast will be high. / Absolute error measures the miss without its sign. / The next-month baseline remains 120 units. / Sales proposes a 30-unit promotional uplift. / That produces a 150-unit scenario, not confirmed demand. / What order or response evidence supports the proposed increment? / A future promotion does not change the earlier forecast errors. / Compare the forecast version that existed at the agreed planning point. / A sales target is not automatically an unbiased demand estimate. / Is the uplift additional demand or partly shifted from another item? / Keep the adjustment, owner, and rationale visible. / Assess whether the adjustment improves the defined forecast measure. / Present baseline, upside, and supporting evidence separately.
Vocabulary: demand forecast: An estimate of future demand for a defined item and period. / actual demand: The observed demand under the stated measurement definition. / forecast bias: A tendency for forecasts to be systematically too high or too low. / signed error: The forecast difference calculated with an explicitly stated direction. / mean error: The average signed forecast error across the defined observations. / absolute error: The size of a forecast error without its positive or negative sign. / mean absolute error: The average absolute error across the defined observations. / overforecast: A forecast greater than the corresponding actual demand. / underforecast: A forecast below the corresponding actual demand. / baseline forecast: The reference forecast before a separately identified adjustment. / promotional uplift: Additional demand expected because of a promotion. / incremental demand: Demand additional to the specified base rather than displaced from elsewhere. / cannibalization: A gain in one product's demand at the expense of another's. / seasonality: A recurring demand pattern tied to a calendar or cycle. / trend: A sustained directional movement in a series over time. / forecast horizon: The future period covered by a forecast. / forecast lag: The time between making a forecast and the period being forecast. / forecast snapshot: A saved version of the forecast at a specified time. / consensus forecast: A forecast agreed through the relevant cross-functional planning process. / demand signal: Information indicating demand behavior or potential change. / booked order: An order recorded under the organization's order definition. / scenario assumption: A premise used to construct a possible future case. / forecast override: A deliberate adjustment to a model or baseline forecast. / forecast value added: Improvement in a defined forecast measure from a specified planning step.
Speaking task: Read the corrected forecast dialogue in pairs. Stress plus twenty, mean, and absolute when comparing the historical errors. Switch roles. Read turns 9-20, distinguishing the 120-unit baseline, proposed 30-unit uplift, and 150-unit scenario. Complete and check the transfer. Read 80 minus 100 as minus 20, and keep the 105-unit next-month scenario separate from orders.
Partner: Aisha: Demand planner / Martin: Sales lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A forecast is 80 units and actual demand is 100. Error is defined as forecast minus actual. Next month has a 90-unit baseline and a proposed 15-unit uplift without orders.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Supply Chain and Logistics English
Professional audience: supply-chain managers, planners, buyers, logistics coordinators, warehouse leaders, procurement teams, customs staff, and operations managers
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not customs, legal, financial, transport-safety, or inventory-policy advice. All figures, shipments, organizations, and negotiations are invented. Use applicable law, governing contracts, current trade rules, authorized decisions, and qualified review. US customs examples are identified; requirements differ by jurisdiction.
Lesson 01: Demand Planning and Forecast Bias
Original fictional case: Demand planner Aisha and sales lead Martin review monthly forecasts of 120, 130, and 140 units against verified demand of 100, 110, and 120. For this case, signed error means forecast minus actual demand. Next month's baseline is 120 units. Sales proposes a 30-unit promotional uplift but has no order evidence for it. Aisha and Martin must describe the three-month pattern and preserve the promotion as a scenario without treating either historical bias or future uplift as a certainty forever.
Language workshop: Three optimistic forecasts and a new promotion
Communication goal: Explain signed forecast error, distinguish bias from error size, and keep an unconfirmed promotional uplift separate from the baseline.
Language guidance: With error defined as forecast minus actual, the three errors are +20, +20, and +20 units. Total error is +60 and mean error is +20. Positive means overforecasting under this convention; other systems may reverse the sign.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Here we define signed error as forecast minus actual demand. / Each of the three forecasts exceeded actual demand by 20 units. / Mean signed error is plus 20 units over these three months. / This pattern does not prove every future forecast will be high. / Absolute error measures the miss without its sign. / The next-month baseline remains 120 units. / Sales proposes a 30-unit promotional uplift. / That produces a 150-unit scenario, not confirmed demand. / What order or response evidence supports the proposed increment? / A future promotion does not change the earlier forecast errors. / Compare the forecast version that existed at the agreed planning point. / A sales target is not automatically an unbiased demand estimate. / Is the uplift additional demand or partly shifted from another item? / Keep the adjustment, owner, and rationale visible. / Assess whether the adjustment improves the defined forecast measure. / Present baseline, upside, and supporting evidence separately.
Vocabulary: demand forecast: An estimate of future demand for a defined item and period. / actual demand: The observed demand under the stated measurement definition. / forecast bias: A tendency for forecasts to be systematically too high or too low. / signed error: The forecast difference calculated with an explicitly stated direction. / mean error: The average signed forecast error across the defined observations. / absolute error: The size of a forecast error without its positive or negative sign. / mean absolute error: The average absolute error across the defined observations. / overforecast: A forecast greater than the corresponding actual demand. / underforecast: A forecast below the corresponding actual demand. / baseline forecast: The reference forecast before a separately identified adjustment. / promotional uplift: Additional demand expected because of a promotion. / incremental demand: Demand additional to the specified base rather than displaced from elsewhere. / cannibalization: A gain in one product's demand at the expense of another's. / seasonality: A recurring demand pattern tied to a calendar or cycle. / trend: A sustained directional movement in a series over time. / forecast horizon: The future period covered by a forecast. / forecast lag: The time between making a forecast and the period being forecast. / forecast snapshot: A saved version of the forecast at a specified time. / consensus forecast: A forecast agreed through the relevant cross-functional planning process. / demand signal: Information indicating demand behavior or potential change. / booked order: An order recorded under the organization's order definition. / scenario assumption: A premise used to construct a possible future case. / forecast override: A deliberate adjustment to a model or baseline forecast. / forecast value added: Improvement in a defined forecast measure from a specified planning step.
Speaking task: Read the corrected forecast dialogue in pairs. Stress plus twenty, mean, and absolute when comparing the historical errors. Switch roles. Read turns 9-20, distinguishing the 120-unit baseline, proposed 30-unit uplift, and 150-unit scenario. Complete and check the transfer. Read 80 minus 100 as minus 20, and keep the 105-unit next-month scenario separate from orders.
Partner: Aisha: Demand planner / Martin: Sales lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A forecast is 80 units and actual demand is 100. Error is defined as forecast minus actual. Next month has a 90-unit baseline and a proposed 15-unit uplift without orders.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Martin: When the next results arrive, let's evaluate the forecast version we actually used. Revising old numbers after seeing demand would make the performance comparison misleading.
Aisha: Preserve the forecast snapshot and its planning date. We need a consistent lag and definition if we want to learn whether the adjustment helped.
Martin: I will present one hundred twenty as baseline and one hundred fifty as the promotion scenario, with the evidence gap stated. The historical errors will remain unchanged.
Aisha: Good. Record the proposed forecast override with its owner and rationale. That gives the opportunity a fair assessment without disguising uncertainty or treating the scenario as a customer commitment.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

02Procurement and Supplier PerformanceAcknowledged, but not scheduled+

Your goal

Request a usable supplier commitment by separating acknowledgment, quantities, shipment timing, arrival timing, and unresolved dependencies.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Acknowledged, but not scheduled

Buyer Keiko and supplier account manager Luis discuss purchase order P46 for 500 units. The supplier has acknowledged receipt but has given no shipping date. The buyer needs warehouse receipt by October 12. Capacity and transit timing are not confirmed. A supplier message promises an update tomorrow at noon, not dispatch tomorrow. Keiko must obtain a clear status and request line-level quantities and dates without treating the acknowledgment or update appointment as a delivery commitment.

Keiko: Buyer / Luis: Supplier account manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has the supplier confirmed?

Answer and explanation

A. Receipt of order P46, not a shipping date

The brief supplies acknowledgment only and explicitly leaves timing and capacity unconfirmed.

2. What does October 12 refer to?

Answer and explanation

C. The buyer's required warehouse-receipt date

The requested event is receipt at the buyer's warehouse, which differs from dispatch.

3. What is promised for tomorrow at noon?

Answer and explanation

C. An update

The message schedules a status response, not a physical movement of the goods.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Order acknowledged does not specify which dates or quantities have been accepted. Ask what the acknowledgment covers. A requested warehouse-receipt date needs a compatible dispatch plan and transit basis; neither is established in this case.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
procurement
The process of sourcing and obtaining required goods or services.coordinate procurement
purchase order
A buyer's order identifying goods, quantities, and relevant terms.issue a purchase order
order acknowledgment
Confirmation that an order has been received or accepted as stated.clarify the order acknowledgment
requested date
The date a party asks another party to meet.distinguish requested and committed dates
committed date
A date a party has explicitly agreed to meet within stated terms.obtain a committed date
ship date
The date goods leave the specified shipping point.confirm the ship date
receipt date
The date goods arrive and are received at the stated location.specify the required receipt date
supplier lead time
The time between defined order and supply events.verify supplier lead time
transit time
The time spent moving goods between specified points.confirm transit time
order line
A separate item-and-quantity entry within an order.review each order line
partial shipment
Delivery of only part of the ordered quantity in one dispatch.propose a partial shipment
backorder
Ordered demand that cannot yet be fulfilled as required.track a backorder
allocation
Assignment of limited supply to particular orders or customers.confirm supply allocation
capacity commitment
A confirmed assignment of production capability for a specified need.secure a capacity commitment
expediting
Action to accelerate or closely follow a delayed supply activity.request expediting
minimum order quantity
The smallest quantity a supplier will accept under specified terms.review the minimum order quantity
request for quotation
A buyer's request for a priced offer against defined requirements.issue a request for quotation
quotation validity
The period or conditions under which a supplier's offer remains open.confirm quotation validity
supplier scorecard
A structured summary of supplier performance against defined measures.review the supplier scorecard
on-time delivery
Delivery within the agreed timing definition and tolerance.define on-time delivery
on-time in-full
Delivery meeting both the agreed timing and quantity requirements.measure on-time in-full
service-level agreement
An agreement defining specified service commitments and measures.review the service-level agreement
commercial escalation
Referral of an unresolved supply issue to the appropriate decision level.initiate a commercial escalation
status checkpoint
An agreed point for obtaining an updated status.set a status checkpoint

Tomorrow at noon is a status checkpoint, not a ship date. A proposed partial shipment must identify quantities, dates, and any remainder. Do not improve a supplier score by replacing the agreed performance basis after an order is late.

C Notice the language

A precise request is easier to answer

Instead of asking whether an order is on track, identify the quantity, location, event, and date you need. Separate a request for information from a claim that the supplier has already promised it. This keeps a firm commercial discussion accurate even when the missing date is inconvenient.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the order
This concerns P46 for 500 units.
Clarify acknowledgment
Does your acknowledgment confirm receipt only, or specific quantities and dates?
State the required event
We need warehouse receipt by October 12.
Separate dispatch
A ship date is not the same as a warehouse-receipt date.
Request the commitment
Please confirm the quantity and committed ship date for each line.
Identify missing capacity
Is capacity allocated to this order, or still under review?
Ask about transit
What transit assumption supports the proposed arrival?
Clarify the update
Tomorrow at noon is the response time, not a dispatch commitment.
Keep planning honest
We cannot enter a confirmed receipt date without a supported supply plan.
Explore a partial
A partial shipment may help if quantities and the remainder are explicit.
Preserve the total
Show how the proposed shipments add up to all 500 units.
Ask for the constraint
What prevents you from confirming the date now?
Escalate constructively
Please involve the person who can confirm allocation and timing.
Define the measure
Are we scoring departure or receipt, and against which agreed date?
Avoid an invented promise
I will report the date as unconfirmed until your commitment is clear.
Close the checkpoint
Please send the status tomorrow at noon even if the final date remains open.
6 language and precision notes
Acknowledged
Can mean received rather than scheduled; ask what is confirmed.
By
Sets a latest time for a specified event; name the event.
Committed
More definite than proposed or estimated, but still governed by the actual agreement.
Partial
Requires a quantity and a plan for what remains.
On time
Needs the agreed date, event, location, and tolerance.
Update
A communication event, not a physical shipment milestone.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which internal status is accurate now?

Answer and explanation

C. P46 acknowledged; ship date, capacity, and transit basis unconfirmed.

The status distinguishes known acknowledgment from the unresolved planning inputs without inventing a promise or breach.

2. Which request would best clarify the missing commitment?

Answer and explanation

C. Please identify the quantity, departure location, committed ship date, and transit basis for warehouse receipt.

Quantity, event, location, timing, and transit assumptions address the actual planning gap; a receipt acknowledgment or response time does not.

3. The supplier proposes 200 units first and 300 later. What still needs clarification?

Answer and explanation

B. Dates, locations, terms, and status for both shipments

The quantities reconcile, but a usable plan still needs the timing and conditions of both movements.

4. How should tomorrow's noon appointment be reported?

Answer and explanation

A. Supplier status update due; shipment date still unconfirmed

The supplied promise concerns communication and must not be relabeled as a shipment event.

E Conversations

Acknowledged, but not scheduled

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Keiko: Buyer / Luis: Supplier account manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • order line
  • order acknowledgment
  • status checkpoint
  • receipt date
  • partial shipment
  • committed date
  • capacity commitment
  • On-time delivery
  • commercial escalation
  • transit time
  1. Keiko
    Thank you for acknowledging P46. We ordered five hundred units and need them at our warehouse by October twelve, but your message does not include a ship date.
  2. Luis
    The 1 confirms receipt of the order. It does not yet confirm allocation or dispatch timing, and I do not want you to mistake it for that commitment.
  3. Keiko
    Just to be clear, October twelve is our required warehouse receipt date. A shipment leaving your site that day would not meet the request.
  4. Luis
    Understood. That is your required 2. We need a compatible dispatch plan and transit basis before I can tell you whether we can support it.
  5. Keiko
    Your note also says tomorrow at noon. Our planner read that as the time the order would ship, but I think you meant the next response.
  6. Luis
    Correct. It is a 3. I will provide an update then; it is not a promise that five hundred units will leave at noon.
  7. Keiko
    Please call at noon even if the date is still open. Tell us what is unresolved and who is working on it so we can update production.
  8. Luis
    I will. The 4 is still under review. I need the responsible planning team to confirm what capacity can be assigned to this order.
  9. Keiko
    When you have that assessment, please identify the quantity and timing for each item line. A single general date could hide different availability across the order.
  10. Luis
    I will report each 5 separately where timing differs. The total needs to remain five hundred, with any split clearly linked to the original order quantities.
  11. Keiko
    A split could help our operations, but only if it is a real proposal with dates. Two hundred first and three hundred later would not be enough by itself.
  12. Luis
    Yes. A 6 needs quantities, timing, and the remaining balance. I will not describe a possible split as though it has already been approved or dispatched.
  13. Keiko
    We also need to know the dispatch location and what the transport estimate includes. Otherwise a proposed departure date may not support the required warehouse arrival.
  14. Luis
    The 7 basis must be explicit. We should identify the relevant route and conditions rather than assume a generic number covers every movement and receiving step.
  15. Keiko
    Then we can distinguish proposals from commitments. Our internal schedule should use the same status wording.
  16. Luis
    Exactly. I will label a 8 only when the relevant commitment is established. Until then, the requested warehouse date and our unconfirmed supply timing must remain separate.
  17. Keiko
    If the planning team cannot resolve allocation, please involve the right decision maker. We need a practical next route without accusing anyone of breaking a date not yet promised.
  18. Luis
    I will use the 9 process if needed. The update will identify the constraint and owner rather than replace the missing date with a vague assurance.
  19. Keiko
    For performance reporting later, let's also agree whether we measure dispatch or warehouse receipt. Moving between those definitions would make on-time performance misleading.
  20. Luis
    Agreed. 10 needs a fixed basis. For now, report P46 as acknowledged with timing unconfirmed, and expect my status response tomorrow at noon.

Answers and explanations
  1. order acknowledgment Turn 2. The acknowledgment confirms receipt of P46 here, not a shipping or delivery date.
  2. receipt date Turn 4. The receipt date concerns arrival and receipt at the buyer's warehouse, distinct from departure from the supplier.
  3. status checkpoint Turn 6. The noon checkpoint is a promise to provide information, not a promise that the goods will move then.
  4. capacity commitment Turn 8. The capacity commitment is not yet confirmed, so production capability cannot be treated as assigned to P46.
  5. order line Turn 10. An order line identifies a specific item and quantity, allowing different availability to be stated precisely.
  6. partial shipment Turn 12. A partial shipment sends only part of the order and requires a clear plan for both that quantity and the remainder.
  7. transit time Turn 14. Transit time describes movement between specified points and must be defined before deriving an arrival plan.
  8. committed date Turn 16. A committed date is an explicit agreed commitment, distinct from the buyer's request or an unconfirmed estimate.
  9. commercial escalation Turn 18. Commercial escalation routes the unresolved allocation and timing question to someone with the appropriate decision authority.
  10. On-time delivery Turn 20. On-time delivery needs a defined event, agreed date, location, and tolerance rather than a changing interpretation after the event.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Keiko: Thank you for acknowledging P46. We ordered five hundred units and need them at our warehouse by October twelve, but your message does not include a ship date.
  2. Luis: The order acknowledgment confirms receipt of the order. It does not yet confirm allocation or dispatch timing, and I do not want you to mistake it for that commitment.
  3. Keiko: Just to be clear, October twelve is our required warehouse receipt date. A shipment leaving your site that day would not meet the request.
  4. Luis: Understood. That is your required receipt date. We need a compatible dispatch plan and transit basis before I can tell you whether we can support it.
  5. Keiko: Your note also says tomorrow at noon. Our planner read that as the time the order would ship, but I think you meant the next response.
  6. Luis: Correct. It is a status checkpoint. I will provide an update then; it is not a promise that five hundred units will leave at noon.
  7. Keiko: Please call at noon even if the date is still open. Tell us what is unresolved and who is working on it so we can update production.
  8. Luis: I will. The capacity commitment is still under review. I need the responsible planning team to confirm what capacity can be assigned to this order.
  9. Keiko: When you have that assessment, please identify the quantity and timing for each item line. A single general date could hide different availability across the order.
  10. Luis: I will report each order line separately where timing differs. The total needs to remain five hundred, with any split clearly linked to the original order quantities.
  11. Keiko: A split could help our operations, but only if it is a real proposal with dates. Two hundred first and three hundred later would not be enough by itself.
  12. Luis: Yes. A partial shipment needs quantities, timing, and the remaining balance. I will not describe a possible split as though it has already been approved or dispatched.
  13. Keiko: We also need to know the dispatch location and what the transport estimate includes. Otherwise a proposed departure date may not support the required warehouse arrival.
  14. Luis: The transit time basis must be explicit. We should identify the relevant route and conditions rather than assume a generic number covers every movement and receiving step.
  15. Keiko: Then we can distinguish proposals from commitments. Our internal schedule should use the same status wording.
  16. Luis: Exactly. I will label a committed date only when the relevant commitment is established. Until then, the requested warehouse date and our unconfirmed supply timing must remain separate.
  17. Keiko: If the planning team cannot resolve allocation, please involve the right decision maker. We need a practical next route without accusing anyone of breaking a date not yet promised.
  18. Luis: I will use the commercial escalation process if needed. The update will identify the constraint and owner rather than replace the missing date with a vague assurance.
  19. Keiko: For performance reporting later, let's also agree whether we measure dispatch or warehouse receipt. Moving between those definitions would make on-time performance misleading.
  20. Luis: Agreed. On-time delivery needs a fixed basis. For now, report P46 as acknowledged with timing unconfirmed, and expect my status response tomorrow at noon.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An acknowledgment without a shipping date

Fictional procurement call: the supplier acknowledged purchase order 612 but provided no shipping date; the planning review is tomorrow afternoon.

  1. Buyer: Thank you for acknowledging purchase order six twelve. Please confirm the shipping date or current constraint.
  2. Supplier coordinator: The acknowledgment confirms receipt of the order, not a committed dispatch date yet.
  3. Buyer: We need a date for tomorrow afternoon's planning review. Can you check and return a status before noon?
  4. Supplier coordinator: I can check with planning this morning and confirm when a reliable update is available.
  5. Buyer: Please distinguish a firm commitment from an estimated lead time in that response.
  6. Supplier coordinator: Understood. I won't turn the standard lead time into a confirmed shipping promise.
  7. Buyer: If allocation is limiting the order, please identify the affected quantity rather than simply say delayed.
  8. Supplier coordinator: I'll ask for that detail, but the current cause isn't confirmed, so I won't assume allocation.
  9. Buyer: Thank you. I'll keep our downstream schedule provisional until we receive the verified status.
  10. Supplier coordinator: Request accepted. I'll return the planning response or explain the remaining uncertainty before your review.
A partial shipment needs a quantity and date

Separate fictional supplier case: order 733 is for 400 units; the supplier offers a partial shipment without specifying quantity or dispatch date.

  1. Procurement specialist: You offered a partial shipment against the four hundred units on order seven thirty-three. Please specify quantity and dispatch date.
  2. Supplier account manager: Those details aren't confirmed yet. I can ask our allocation team for the available quantity.
  3. Procurement specialist: Could you also confirm the balance plan so we can assess the offer before accepting it?
  4. Supplier account manager: Yes. Would you accept any available quantity now and leave the remainder open?
  5. Procurement specialist: We need the actual split and timing first; otherwise we can't evaluate the impact on our schedule.
  6. Supplier account manager: I'll request both the proposed first shipment and the remaining commitment from planning.
  7. Procurement specialist: Please return an initial status today, even if the quantities remain unconfirmed.
  8. Supplier account manager: I can provide that status by the end of our working day without promising shipment.
  9. Procurement specialist: Agreed. We are requesting details, not accepting an undefined partial-delivery arrangement.
  10. Supplier account manager: Understood. I'll label estimates clearly and wait for your reviewed response to the complete proposal.
Confirm the OTIF measure before the supplier review

Separate fictional performance meeting: the buyer calculates OTIF by order, while the supplier's presentation calculates it by order line; the joint review is Friday.

  1. Supplier performance analyst: Before Friday's review, please confirm the denominator and delivery basis used in your OTIF presentation.
  2. Supplier representative: Our on-time, in-full figure is calculated by order line. Is your report using the same unit?
  3. Supplier performance analyst: No, ours uses whole orders, so the two percentages aren't directly comparable.
  4. Supplier representative: Would you like us to replace our number with yours before the meeting?
  5. Supplier performance analyst: Please provide the underlying order-line data and definition first so we can reconcile both views.
  6. Supplier representative: I can ask our analyst to prepare that. What timing would support your review?
  7. Supplier performance analyst: An initial data-availability confirmation tomorrow would help us agree a realistic reconciliation schedule.
  8. Supplier representative: I'll confirm availability tomorrow, but I can't promise the reconciled calculation before assessing the work.
  9. Supplier performance analyst: That's fine. Keep the current definition visible rather than present either number as the agreed result.
  10. Supplier representative: Request received. We'll distinguish our existing measure from the common basis still to be agreed.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Acknowledged, but not scheduled
  1. Read turns 1-10, clearly distinguishing warehouse receipt by October 12 from tomorrow's noon status response.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress the requested shipment quantities, departure dates, and unconfirmed transit basis.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

An update is not a departure

A supplier acknowledges an order for 80 units. Receipt is requested on Friday. The supplier promises a status response on Tuesday but has not confirmed dispatch.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Buyer: "Friday is our requested receipt ___."

Answer and explanation

C. date

Friday refers to arrival at the buyer's location, not confirmed dispatch.

2. Supplier: "Tuesday is the promised status ___."

Answer and explanation

D. response

The supplier has promised information on Tuesday rather than movement of goods.

3. Buyer: "The ship date remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unconfirmed

No dispatch commitment is supplied in the scenario.

4. Supplier: "Acknowledgment alone does not establish ___."

Answer and explanation

B. delivery

Receiving the order does not demonstrate that the goods were shipped or received.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Conversation explanations in the book, page 88 ↗

Lesson 02: Procurement and Supplier Performance
Original fictional case: Buyer Keiko and supplier account manager Luis discuss purchase order P46 for 500 units. The supplier has acknowledged receipt but has given no shipping date. The buyer needs warehouse receipt by October 12. Capacity and transit timing are not confirmed. A supplier message promises an update tomorrow at noon, not dispatch tomorrow. Keiko must obtain a clear status and request line-level quantities and dates without treating the acknowledgment or update appointment as a delivery commitment.
Language workshop: Acknowledged, but not scheduled
Communication goal: Request a usable supplier commitment by separating acknowledgment, quantities, shipment timing, arrival timing, and unresolved dependencies.
Language guidance: Order acknowledged does not specify which dates or quantities have been accepted. Ask what the acknowledgment covers. A requested warehouse-receipt date needs a compatible dispatch plan and transit basis; neither is established in this case.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This concerns P46 for 500 units. / Does your acknowledgment confirm receipt only, or specific quantities and dates? / We need warehouse receipt by October 12. / A ship date is not the same as a warehouse-receipt date. / Please confirm the quantity and committed ship date for each line. / Is capacity allocated to this order, or still under review? / What transit assumption supports the proposed arrival? / Tomorrow at noon is the response time, not a dispatch commitment. / We cannot enter a confirmed receipt date without a supported supply plan. / A partial shipment may help if quantities and the remainder are explicit. / Show how the proposed shipments add up to all 500 units. / What prevents you from confirming the date now? / Please involve the person who can confirm allocation and timing. / Are we scoring departure or receipt, and against which agreed date? / I will report the date as unconfirmed until your commitment is clear. / Please send the status tomorrow at noon even if the final date remains open.
Vocabulary: procurement: The process of sourcing and obtaining required goods or services. / purchase order: A buyer's order identifying goods, quantities, and relevant terms. / order acknowledgment: Confirmation that an order has been received or accepted as stated. / requested date: The date a party asks another party to meet. / committed date: A date a party has explicitly agreed to meet within stated terms. / ship date: The date goods leave the specified shipping point. / receipt date: The date goods arrive and are received at the stated location. / supplier lead time: The time between defined order and supply events. / transit time: The time spent moving goods between specified points. / order line: A separate item-and-quantity entry within an order. / partial shipment: Delivery of only part of the ordered quantity in one dispatch. / backorder: Ordered demand that cannot yet be fulfilled as required. / allocation: Assignment of limited supply to particular orders or customers. / capacity commitment: A confirmed assignment of production capability for a specified need. / expediting: Action to accelerate or closely follow a delayed supply activity. / minimum order quantity: The smallest quantity a supplier will accept under specified terms. / request for quotation: A buyer's request for a priced offer against defined requirements. / quotation validity: The period or conditions under which a supplier's offer remains open. / supplier scorecard: A structured summary of supplier performance against defined measures. / on-time delivery: Delivery within the agreed timing definition and tolerance. / on-time in-full: Delivery meeting both the agreed timing and quantity requirements. / service-level agreement: An agreement defining specified service commitments and measures. / commercial escalation: Referral of an unresolved supply issue to the appropriate decision level. / status checkpoint: An agreed point for obtaining an updated status.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, clearly distinguishing warehouse receipt by October 12 from tomorrow's noon status response. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress the requested shipment quantities, departure dates, and unconfirmed transit basis. Complete and check the transfer. Repeat Tuesday as an update commitment and Friday as requested receipt, without adding a dispatch promise.
Partner: Keiko: Buyer / Luis: Supplier account manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A supplier acknowledges an order for 80 units. Receipt is requested on Friday. The supplier promises a status response on Tuesday but has not confirmed dispatch.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Keiko: If the planning team cannot resolve allocation, please involve the right decision maker. We need a practical next route without accusing anyone of breaking a date not yet promised.
Luis: I will use the commercial escalation process if needed. The update will identify the constraint and owner rather than replace the missing date with a vague assurance.
Keiko: For performance reporting later, let's also agree whether we measure dispatch or warehouse receipt. Moving between those definitions would make on-time performance misleading.
Luis: Agreed. On-time delivery needs a fixed basis. For now, report P46 as acknowledged with timing unconfirmed, and expect my status response tomorrow at noon.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
