Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Wednesday is an estimate before departure

Fictional freight call: the carrier estimates Wednesday delivery but has not confirmed departure; the customer requests a guaranteed arrival time.

  1. Customer service manager: The customer wants a guaranteed arrival time. Can we promise Wednesday based on the carrier's ETA?
  2. Transport planner: Wednesday is an estimate, and departure hasn't been confirmed, so I can't guarantee arrival.
  3. Customer service manager: Does the estimate at least establish that the shipment is already moving?
  4. Transport planner: No. An estimated arrival and a verified departure are different pieces of information.
  5. Customer service manager: I'll say Wednesday is the current estimate, subject to confirmation, with departure still unverified.
  6. Transport planner: That matches what we know. We can request a departure update from the carrier.
  7. Customer service manager: Would asking to expedite automatically make the original estimate a guaranteed commitment?
  8. Transport planner: No. Any alternative service and its conditions would need confirmation before we communicate them.
  9. Customer service manager: I'll keep the customer's urgency visible without making a promise the evidence doesn't support.
  10. Transport planner: I'll return the verified carrier status and distinguish any revised estimate from a firm service commitment.
A tracking scan is not proof of delivery

Separate fictional shipment case: tracking shows arrival at a regional hub at 06:00; no delivery scan or recipient confirmation exists.

  1. Account coordinator: The shipment arrived at six. Can I tell the customer delivery is complete?
  2. Freight specialist: The scan says regional hub arrival at six, not arrival at the customer's location.
  3. Account coordinator: I shortened the status too far. What can we state without guessing the remaining route?
  4. Freight specialist: It reached the regional hub; final delivery is unconfirmed in the available tracking information.
  5. Account coordinator: Does the missing delivery scan prove the carrier failed to deliver it?
  6. Freight specialist: Not necessarily. We need confirmation rather than infer either completion or failure from the missing scan.
  7. Account coordinator: I'll correct my message and ask for the latest delivery status and ETA.
  8. Freight specialist: Please keep the scan location and time attached so the source of the update is clear.
  9. Account coordinator: I won't invent an out-for-delivery status to reassure the customer while we wait.
  10. Freight specialist: Good. I'll contact the carrier and relay confirmed events separately from estimates or unresolved tracking gaps.
An expedite quote is only one part of the option

Separate fictional freight exercise: a carrier quotes an expedited service price but has not confirmed collection capacity or delivery commitment.

  1. Purchasing manager: We have an expedite price. Can I tell operations the delay is solved if we accept it?
  2. Transport coordinator: Not yet. Collection capacity and the delivery commitment remain unconfirmed, despite the quoted price.
  3. Purchasing manager: So a price offer doesn't establish that the carrier can execute the proposed movement?
  4. Transport coordinator: Correct. We need the operational terms as well as the cost before describing the option as available.
  5. Purchasing manager: Could we assume the existing incoterms answer every responsibility question for the expedited arrangement?
  6. Transport coordinator: We should check the actual agreed terms and proposed change rather than assume their application.
  7. Purchasing manager: I'll describe the quote as received, with service availability and responsibilities still under review.
  8. Transport coordinator: Please avoid promising a new ETA until the carrier provides the relevant confirmation.
  9. Purchasing manager: I'll request that information before asking the decision maker to approve the option.
  10. Transport coordinator: I'll assess the returned service details and separate a possible recovery route from a verified transport commitment.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Wednesday is still an estimate
  1. Read turns 1-10, keeping Wednesday's arrival estimate separate from the customer's Wednesday 14:00 production requirement.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress the unresolved cost, booking, and departure conditions and Tuesday's 16:00 update.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A scheduled pickup is not a completed pickup

Pickup is scheduled for Monday. Arrival is estimated Thursday. No collection event is confirmed, and the next update is Monday at 15:00.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "Monday's pickup is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. scheduled

A planned pickup date does not establish that collection already occurred.

2. Customer: "Thursday's arrival remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. estimated

The arrival is a forecast rather than a confirmed or guaranteed event.

3. Coordinator: "Actual collection is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

B. confirmed

The case supplies no evidence of a completed collection event.

4. Customer: "Fifteen hundred is the next status ___."

Answer and explanation

A. update

The time concerns communication, not guaranteed collection or arrival.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 05: Freight, Routing, and Carrier Delays
Original fictional case: Logistics coordinator Nikhil and customer planner Dana review a fictional shipment. The carrier currently estimates warehouse arrival on Wednesday, but departure has not been confirmed. Dana needs the goods for a Wednesday 14:00 production start and asks for a guarantee. An expedited route is only being assessed; its capacity, extra cost, and arrival timing are unconfirmed. Nikhil has arranged a carrier status update for Tuesday at 16:00. He must state what is known without treating that update or the alternative route as a delivery promise.
Language workshop: Wednesday is still an estimate
Communication goal: Give a useful delay update while separating a carrier estimate, confirmed transport events, appointment timing, and an authorized contingency.
Language guidance: An ETA is an estimate for a stated event and place. It is not proof of departure or a guarantee that goods will be usable for a production start. A warehouse appointment and a confirmed arrival are also different events.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The carrier currently estimates warehouse arrival on Wednesday. / Departure has not yet been confirmed. / I cannot confirm arrival before your 14:00 production start from the available information. / I understand that the uncertainty affects your production plan. / This estimate refers to your warehouse, not merely the destination terminal. / Please provide the confirmed departure event and its source. / A receiving slot is not proof that the vehicle will arrive in that slot. / We are assessing expedited routing, not reporting a completed booking. / Any premium cost needs the appropriate authorization. / Space must be confirmed before we rely on the alternative. / The next carrier status update is Tuesday at 16:00. / I will update you then even if the arrival remains uncertain. / A forecast departure is not actual departure. / The agreed trade rule does not itself guarantee Wednesday arrival. / State which milestone, estimate, or condition has changed. / Use Wednesday as the current estimate, with the departure dependency explicit.
Vocabulary: estimated time of arrival: A forecast arrival time for a specified destination, abbreviated ETA. / estimated time of departure: A forecast departure time from a specified location, abbreviated ETD. / actual departure: The confirmed time the shipment left the stated location. / actual arrival: The confirmed time the shipment reached the stated point. / carrier: The organization transporting goods under the relevant arrangement. / freight forwarder: A party arranging transport and related logistics services. / consignor: The party sending goods under the transport record. / consignee: The party named to receive goods under the transport record. / bill of lading: A transport document evidencing receipt and carriage terms, with functions varying by type. / air waybill: A nonnegotiable document used for air-cargo carriage. / transport milestone: A defined event used to track shipment progress. / tracking exception: A reported departure from the expected shipment process. / transshipment: Transfer of goods between transport services during a journey. / routing: The planned path and transport connections for a shipment. / last mile: The final transport leg to the delivery destination. / delivery appointment: An agreed receiving time slot at the destination. / proof of delivery: Evidence documenting the defined delivery event. / expedited freight: Transport arranged to reduce delivery time under specified conditions. / premium freight cost: Additional transport cost for a higher-cost or urgent service. / capacity booking: A reservation of transport space under the applicable terms. / demurrage: A charge for specified excess terminal or port use under applicable terms. / detention: A charge for specified extended equipment use under applicable terms. / Incoterms rule: An ICC trade rule allocating specified delivery obligations, costs, and risks. / named place: The specific location stated with the relevant trade or delivery term.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, keeping Wednesday's arrival estimate separate from the customer's Wednesday 14:00 production requirement. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress the unresolved cost, booking, and departure conditions and Tuesday's 16:00 update. Complete and check the transfer. Contrast scheduled pickup, estimated arrival, and an actual collection not yet confirmed.
Partner: Nikhil: Logistics coordinator / Dana: Customer planner
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Pickup is scheduled for Monday. Arrival is estimated Thursday. No collection event is confirmed, and the next update is Monday at 15:00.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dana: What can you promise about the next communication? We need a reliable checkpoint even if the transport timing remains uncertain.
Nikhil: The carrier update is Tuesday at sixteen hundred. I will request the next confirmed transport milestone and tell you what changed, including if departure or the alternative still remains unresolved.
Dana: That gives us an honest planning basis. We will treat Wednesday as the current estimate, keep the production dependency visible, and wait for the confirmed movement update.
Nikhil: Once delivery occurs, we can obtain proof of delivery. Until then, I will distinguish estimates, bookings, confirmed events, and the next communication rather than combine them into a guarantee.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Customs, Trade Compliance, and DocumentationThe invoice and packing list describe different goods+

Your goal

Raise a document discrepancy, request verified product facts, and arrange controlled correction without guessing classification or changing facts to fit a document.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

The invoice and packing list describe different goods

Trade coordinator Sofia and exporter contact Chen review a fictional US-bound shipment file. The invoice describes stainless-steel bolts; the packing list describes aluminum brackets. Both show 500 pieces, but matching counts do not establish which description is correct. The broker requests clarification before relying on the file. No verified product specification, classification, or origin determination is supplied. Sofia and Chen must obtain the underlying product facts and corrected authorized documents without simply copying one unverified description over the other.

Sofia: Trade coordinator / Chen: Exporter documentation contact

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the discrepancy?

Answer and explanation

A. The invoice describes steel bolts while the packing list describes aluminum brackets.

The document descriptions differ in product and material even though both list five hundred pieces.

2. What do matching counts establish?

Answer and explanation

A. Agreement on the stated count, not verification of the product description

The same number does not resolve a conflict over what the goods actually are.

3. What is missing before an accurate correction?

Answer and explanation

A. Verified product facts and the appropriate document correction process

The file needs underlying evidence and authorized correction rather than selection of a convenient description.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Five hundred pieces on both documents confirms only agreement in the stated count. Bolts and brackets, and stainless steel and aluminum, are materially different descriptions. Neither document is established as correct by the supplied facts.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
commercial invoice
A transaction document describing goods, value, parties, and relevant terms.verify the commercial invoice
packing list
A document describing how goods and quantities are packed.reconcile the packing list
merchandise description
The factual identification and characteristics of the goods.provide a precise merchandise description
product specification
Technical information defining a product's characteristics.obtain the product specification
material composition
The substances or materials making up a product.confirm material composition
end use
The purpose for which the product is intended or used.clarify the end use
tariff classification
Assignment of goods to an applicable tariff category under the governing rules.obtain a classification review
Harmonized System
The international goods-classification framework commonly abbreviated HS.distinguish Harmonized System codes
HTSUS
The Harmonized Tariff Schedule of the United States.consult the applicable HTSUS provision
country of origin
The country determined as the goods' origin under applicable rules.verify country of origin
country of shipment
The country from which goods are dispatched.distinguish country of shipment
customs value
The value determined under the applicable customs valuation rules.review customs value
declared quantity
The quantity stated in the relevant customs or shipment record.verify the declared quantity
unit of measure
The basis used to count or measure goods.confirm the unit of measure
net weight
The weight of goods excluding the specified packaging.state net weight
gross weight
The weight including the specified goods and packaging.distinguish gross weight
importer of record
The party responsible for the import entry under applicable law.identify the importer of record
customs broker
A qualified intermediary conducting customs business under the relevant authorization.coordinate with the customs broker
entry documentation
The records supporting the import entry process.review entry documentation
supporting evidence
Records substantiating the information in a declaration or document.retain supporting evidence
document discrepancy
A conflict between relevant records or stated facts.resolve a document discrepancy
corrected invoice
An authorized revised invoice addressing an identified error.request a corrected invoice
revision history
A record of what changed, when, and why.preserve revision history
customs release
The customs decision permitting goods to proceed under applicable conditions.confirm customs release

Do not choose a tariff category, origin, or description to obtain a preferred duty result. Accurate product facts must support the relevant qualified review. Shipment country is not automatically origin, and a corrected file is not itself proof of customs release.

C Notice the language

Consistency is necessary but not sufficient

Two documents can match and still be wrong. State the discrepancy neutrally, then seek the facts that identify the goods. The goal is an accurate, traceable file, not just matching words that make the broker stop asking questions.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the discrepancy
The invoice says stainless-steel bolts; the packing list says aluminum brackets.
Keep the count separate
Both list 500 pieces, but that does not resolve the product identity.
Avoid choosing by convenience
We cannot assume either description is correct without supporting facts.
Request the specification
Please provide the verified product specification and relevant order details.
Clarify composition
Confirm the actual material rather than copying an unverified label.
Check the packing basis
Reconcile the item identifiers and package contents.
Preserve document authority
Ask the responsible issuer to correct the affected document.
Keep history visible
Retain the original and the reason for the correction.
Separate classification
The product description does not by itself complete a tariff review.
Avoid a duty-driven description
The wording must follow the goods, not a preferred duty outcome.
Distinguish origin
Country of shipment does not automatically establish country of origin.
Coordinate review
Provide the verified facts to the broker and responsible import team.
Do not invent a code
Classification needs the applicable facts and qualified review.
Limit the status
The discrepancy is being resolved; customs release is not confirmed.
Check agreement and truth
The corrected documents must match the verified goods, not merely each other.
Close the handoff
Send the authorized corrections with their supporting references.
6 language and precision notes
Described as
Attributes wording to a document without endorsing its accuracy.
Verified
Requires a source tied to the actual goods.
Matching
Means consistent with another record, not necessarily factually correct.
Origin
A rules-based determination, not simply the dispatch location.
Corrected
Indicates a documented change; it does not imply customs has accepted the entry.
Released
An actual customs status, separate from a completed internal file.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which message to the exporter is most appropriate?

Answer and explanation

A. Please verify the actual product and material, then arrange authorized corrections supported by those facts.

The message seeks accurate underlying facts and controlled correction rather than an arbitrary choice between conflicting records.

2. A colleague changes both descriptions to metal parts without checking the goods. What remains unresolved?

Answer and explanation

A. The actual product and material; matching vague wording does not verify them.

Agreement between documents is not verification against the goods. Replacing conflicting specifics with a vague shared label hides rather than resolves the discrepancy.

3. Which origin statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Shipment country alone does not determine origin; apply the relevant rules to verified facts.

Origin is determined under applicable rules and cannot be inferred solely from dispatch location or convenience.

4. Which completion statement is supported after an authorized correction but before an agency decision?

Answer and explanation

D. The file has been corrected; customs release still needs confirmation.

Internal document correction and an actual customs release are distinct statuses.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

The invoice and packing list describe different goods

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sofia: Trade coordinator / Chen: Exporter documentation contact

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • revision history
  • merchandise description
  • material composition
  • customs broker
  • corrected invoice
  • product specification
  • document discrepancy
  • Country of origin
  • Tariff classification
  • Customs release
  1. Sofia
    The invoice calls these goods stainless-steel bolts, while the packing list says aluminum brackets. The broker has asked us to clarify the file before relying on it.
  2. Chen
    That is a 1. Both documents show five hundred pieces, but the matching count does not tell us which product description is accurate.
  3. Sofia
    Please check the actual shipment before editing either document. I cannot tell the broker which description is correct from these two conflicting records.
  4. Chen
    I will obtain the 2 and reconcile the order and packing records. We need facts linked to this shipment, not a similar product description from another file.
  5. Sofia
    Material is one of the conflicts, not just the item name. Steel bolts and aluminum brackets are not two harmless ways of saying the same thing.
  6. Chen
    We must confirm 3 from the relevant source. I will not infer it from the document we happen to prefer or from the duty result someone expects.
  7. Sofia
    The broker also needs a description that identifies the goods meaningfully. Making both documents say parts would remove the visible conflict while making the file less informative.
  8. Chen
    Agreed. The 4 must follow the verified goods. A generic replacement would not resolve the underlying uncertainty about product identity and characteristics.
  9. Sofia
    Once the facts are established, the responsible issuer should correct whichever document is wrong. We should not silently overwrite an exporter document ourselves.
  10. Chen
    I will arrange a 5 if the invoice needs correction, and the corresponding packing-list correction if needed. The verified facts determine which records must change.
  11. Sofia
    Please retain the original records and the explanation. The next reviewer needs to understand why the descriptions changed instead of seeing an unexplained replacement.
  12. Chen
    We will preserve the 6 and supporting references. Consistent documents are useful only if their consistency reflects the actual shipment rather than a convenient edit.
  13. Sofia
    We also should not invent a tariff code from this short conversation. We do not yet have the verified characteristics needed for the applicable review.
  14. Chen
    Correct. 7 must follow the relevant facts and rules. A desired duty rate is not a reason to change the product description.
  15. Sofia
    The form names the dispatch country. What do we have to support the origin entry? I do not want someone copying the first country they see.
  16. Chen
    Yes. 8 needs the applicable determination. We should not copy the dispatch country into the origin field merely because it is the only country readily visible.
  17. Sofia
    Once the verified information and authorized corrections are ready, I will send them to the broker and responsible import team with the supporting references.
  18. Chen
    That gives the 9 a factual basis for the required review. We should answer the clarification request directly rather than imply that matching counts settle the matter.
  19. Sofia
    The status update will say the document discrepancy is being resolved. Even a corrected file would not justify telling the warehouse the goods are already released.
  20. Chen
    Exactly. 10 must be confirmed as its own event. Our responsibility here is an accurate, authorized, traceable file, without inventing a classification or a clearance outcome.

Answers and explanations
  1. document discrepancy Turn 2. The discrepancy is a conflict in product and material descriptions, not merely a formatting difference.
  2. product specification Turn 4. The product specification can provide verified characteristics of the actual goods to support an accurate correction.
  3. material composition Turn 6. Material composition identifies what the goods are made of, resolving the steel-versus-aluminum conflict with evidence.
  4. merchandise description Turn 8. The merchandise description should accurately identify the goods rather than hide disagreement behind a vague label.
  5. corrected invoice Turn 10. A corrected invoice is an authorized revision if the invoice is affected, not an informal alteration by an unrelated recipient.
  6. revision history Turn 12. Revision history records the original information, authorized change, and reason so the correction remains traceable.
  7. Tariff classification Turn 14. Tariff classification requires the applicable product facts and rules; the conflicting file does not supply a completed determination.
  8. Country of origin Turn 16. Country of origin is determined under applicable rules and is not automatically the country from which the shipment departed.
  9. customs broker Turn 18. The customs broker receives the verified facts and corrected records for the appropriate customs review rather than being asked to guess product identity.
  10. Customs release Turn 20. Customs release is a separate actual customs decision, not the automatic result of correcting an internal document file.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sofia: The invoice calls these goods stainless-steel bolts, while the packing list says aluminum brackets. The broker has asked us to clarify the file before relying on it.
  2. Chen: That is a document discrepancy. Both documents show five hundred pieces, but the matching count does not tell us which product description is accurate.
  3. Sofia: Please check the actual shipment before editing either document. I cannot tell the broker which description is correct from these two conflicting records.
  4. Chen: I will obtain the product specification and reconcile the order and packing records. We need facts linked to this shipment, not a similar product description from another file.
  5. Sofia: Material is one of the conflicts, not just the item name. Steel bolts and aluminum brackets are not two harmless ways of saying the same thing.
  6. Chen: We must confirm material composition from the relevant source. I will not infer it from the document we happen to prefer or from the duty result someone expects.
  7. Sofia: The broker also needs a description that identifies the goods meaningfully. Making both documents say parts would remove the visible conflict while making the file less informative.
  8. Chen: Agreed. The merchandise description must follow the verified goods. A generic replacement would not resolve the underlying uncertainty about product identity and characteristics.
  9. Sofia: Once the facts are established, the responsible issuer should correct whichever document is wrong. We should not silently overwrite an exporter document ourselves.
  10. Chen: I will arrange a corrected invoice if the invoice needs correction, and the corresponding packing-list correction if needed. The verified facts determine which records must change.
  11. Sofia: Please retain the original records and the explanation. The next reviewer needs to understand why the descriptions changed instead of seeing an unexplained replacement.
  12. Chen: We will preserve the revision history and supporting references. Consistent documents are useful only if their consistency reflects the actual shipment rather than a convenient edit.
  13. Sofia: We also should not invent a tariff code from this short conversation. We do not yet have the verified characteristics needed for the applicable review.
  14. Chen: Correct. Tariff classification must follow the relevant facts and rules. A desired duty rate is not a reason to change the product description.
  15. Sofia: The form names the dispatch country. What do we have to support the origin entry? I do not want someone copying the first country they see.
  16. Chen: Yes. Country of origin needs the applicable determination. We should not copy the dispatch country into the origin field merely because it is the only country readily visible.
  17. Sofia: Once the verified information and authorized corrections are ready, I will send them to the broker and responsible import team with the supporting references.
  18. Chen: That gives the customs broker a factual basis for the required review. We should answer the clarification request directly rather than imply that matching counts settle the matter.
  19. Sofia: The status update will say the document discrepancy is being resolved. Even a corrected file would not justify telling the warehouse the goods are already released.
  20. Chen: Exactly. Customs release must be confirmed as its own event. Our responsibility here is an accurate, authorized, traceable file, without inventing a classification or a clearance outcome.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two product descriptions in one shipment file

Fictional trade-document review: the commercial invoice and packing list describe the product differently; the broker requests clarification before proceeding.

  1. Customs broker: The commercial invoice and packing list use different product descriptions. Which description is supported by the source records?
  2. Shipping coordinator: I need the product team to verify that; I shouldn't choose whichever wording looks easier to process.
  3. Customs broker: Please provide the relevant item reference and the verified description through the authorized documentation route.
  4. Shipping coordinator: Would changing both documents to the shorter phrase settle the discrepancy without further review?
  5. Customs broker: Not if the phrase is unverified. Consistent wording still needs to describe the actual goods accurately.
  6. Shipping coordinator: I'll request source confirmation and preserve the conflicting documents for the review.
  7. Customs broker: Also keep any HS-code or country-of-origin question separate unless that information is actually confirmed.
  8. Shipping coordinator: Understood. A description correction doesn't automatically establish the classification or origin.
  9. Customs broker: Please return the verified clarification so the responsible team can assess the next documentation step.
  10. Shipping coordinator: I'll do that and keep shipment status qualified rather than promise clearance before the review.
Shipping location is not automatically country of origin

Separate fictional documentation case: goods ship from warehouse A, but their manufacturing origin is not supplied; the form asks for country of origin.

  1. Export coordinator: The goods leave warehouse A. Can I enter its country as the country of origin?
  2. Trade compliance specialist: Not from shipping location alone. We need the relevant origin information for the actual goods.
  3. Export coordinator: Which source should I request rather than infer origin from the warehouse address?
  4. Trade compliance specialist: Ask the responsible supplier or product records team for the supporting origin documentation and applicable review.
  5. Export coordinator: Could the commercial invoice address settle it even if it is only the seller's office?
  6. Trade compliance specialist: No. A seller address doesn't automatically establish origin either.
  7. Export coordinator: I'll mark the field as requiring verification and send the item references with my request.
  8. Trade compliance specialist: Good. Don't select a country simply to make the documentation look complete.
  9. Export coordinator: I'll ask the broker what verified information is needed before the shipment file proceeds.
  10. Trade compliance specialist: I'll coordinate the review and keep any legal origin determination with the qualified specialists for the actual context.
A code copied from a similar item

Separate fictional classification discussion: a draft invoice copies an HS code from a similar-looking item; material and function details for the new item are incomplete.

  1. Logistics assistant: I copied the HS code from a similar item. Is that enough for this draft invoice?
  2. Trade specialist: Similarity in appearance doesn't confirm classification. What material and function information do we have for the new item?
  3. Logistics assistant: Those details are incomplete, so I can't show that the two items share the relevant characteristics.
  4. Trade specialist: Then request the product information and have the classification reviewed rather than carry the copied code forward as confirmed.
  5. Logistics assistant: Should I ask the broker to choose a code based only on the product photo?
  6. Trade specialist: Ask what supporting information they need; don't assume the photo is sufficient for the assessment.
  7. Logistics assistant: I'll identify the copied entry as unverified and request the missing material and function details.
  8. Trade specialist: Please retain the item reference so the reviewed answer is linked to the correct goods.
  9. Logistics assistant: I'll return the verified information before treating the commercial invoice as finalized.
  10. Trade specialist: Good. We'll keep the clarification process separate from any unsupported assurance about duties or customs clearance.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The invoice and packing list describe different goods
  1. Read turns 1-10. Name both conflicting goods descriptions while keeping the matching count of 500 unchanged.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Emphasize verified facts, authorized correction, and customs release as separate steps.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Match the goods, not just the words

An invoice says plastic housings and the packing list says steel housings. Both list 40 pieces. Actual material and customs release are unconfirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "The counts match, but the ___ differs."

Answer and explanation

A. material

Plastic and steel are different material descriptions despite the matching quantity.

2. Exporter: "We need verified product ___."

Answer and explanation

C. facts

Underlying evidence is needed before selecting or correcting either description.

3. Coordinator: "The responsible issuer must authorize any ___."

Answer and explanation

C. correction

A document change should follow the appropriate authority and preserve traceability.

4. Exporter: "A corrected file does not prove customs ___."

Answer and explanation

B. release

Document correction is distinct from a confirmed customs release decision.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Supply Chain and Logistics English
Professional audience: supply-chain managers, planners, buyers, logistics coordinators, warehouse leaders, procurement teams, customs staff, and operations managers
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not customs, legal, financial, transport-safety, or inventory-policy advice. All figures, shipments, organizations, and negotiations are invented. Use applicable law, governing contracts, current trade rules, authorized decisions, and qualified review. US customs examples are identified; requirements differ by jurisdiction.
Lesson 06: Customs, Trade Compliance, and Documentation
Original fictional case: Trade coordinator Sofia and exporter contact Chen review a fictional US-bound shipment file. The invoice describes stainless-steel bolts; the packing list describes aluminum brackets. Both show 500 pieces, but matching counts do not establish which description is correct. The broker requests clarification before relying on the file. No verified product specification, classification, or origin determination is supplied. Sofia and Chen must obtain the underlying product facts and corrected authorized documents without simply copying one unverified description over the other.
Language workshop: The invoice and packing list describe different goods
Communication goal: Raise a document discrepancy, request verified product facts, and arrange controlled correction without guessing classification or changing facts to fit a document.
Language guidance: Five hundred pieces on both documents confirms only agreement in the stated count. Bolts and brackets, and stainless steel and aluminum, are materially different descriptions. Neither document is established as correct by the supplied facts.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The invoice says stainless-steel bolts; the packing list says aluminum brackets. / Both list 500 pieces, but that does not resolve the product identity. / We cannot assume either description is correct without supporting facts. / Please provide the verified product specification and relevant order details. / Confirm the actual material rather than copying an unverified label. / Reconcile the item identifiers and package contents. / Ask the responsible issuer to correct the affected document. / Retain the original and the reason for the correction. / The product description does not by itself complete a tariff review. / The wording must follow the goods, not a preferred duty outcome. / Country of shipment does not automatically establish country of origin. / Provide the verified facts to the broker and responsible import team. / Classification needs the applicable facts and qualified review. / The discrepancy is being resolved; customs release is not confirmed. / The corrected documents must match the verified goods, not merely each other. / Send the authorized corrections with their supporting references.
Vocabulary: commercial invoice: A transaction document describing goods, value, parties, and relevant terms. / packing list: A document describing how goods and quantities are packed. / merchandise description: The factual identification and characteristics of the goods. / product specification: Technical information defining a product's characteristics. / material composition: The substances or materials making up a product. / end use: The purpose for which the product is intended or used. / tariff classification: Assignment of goods to an applicable tariff category under the governing rules. / Harmonized System: The international goods-classification framework commonly abbreviated HS. / HTSUS: The Harmonized Tariff Schedule of the United States. / country of origin: The country determined as the goods' origin under applicable rules. / country of shipment: The country from which goods are dispatched. / customs value: The value determined under the applicable customs valuation rules. / declared quantity: The quantity stated in the relevant customs or shipment record. / unit of measure: The basis used to count or measure goods. / net weight: The weight of goods excluding the specified packaging. / gross weight: The weight including the specified goods and packaging. / importer of record: The party responsible for the import entry under applicable law. / customs broker: A qualified intermediary conducting customs business under the relevant authorization. / entry documentation: The records supporting the import entry process. / supporting evidence: Records substantiating the information in a declaration or document. / document discrepancy: A conflict between relevant records or stated facts. / corrected invoice: An authorized revised invoice addressing an identified error. / revision history: A record of what changed, when, and why. / customs release: The customs decision permitting goods to proceed under applicable conditions.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Name both conflicting goods descriptions while keeping the matching count of 500 unchanged. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Emphasize verified facts, authorized correction, and customs release as separate steps. Complete and check the transfer. Read the material discrepancy and correction request without choosing plastic or steel as the verified fact.
Partner: Sofia: Trade coordinator / Chen: Exporter documentation contact
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An invoice says plastic housings and the packing list says steel housings. Both list 40 pieces. Actual material and customs release are unconfirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Sofia: Once the verified information and authorized corrections are ready, I will send them to the broker and responsible import team with the supporting references.
Chen: That gives the customs broker a factual basis for the required review. We should answer the clarification request directly rather than imply that matching counts settle the matter.
Sofia: The status update will say the document discrepancy is being resolved. Even a corrected file would not justify telling the warehouse the goods are already released.
Chen: Exactly. Customs release must be confirmed as its own event. Our responsibility here is an accurate, authorized, traceable file, without inventing a classification or a clearance outcome.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Chen: That gives the customs broker a factual basis for the required review. We should answer the clarification request directly rather than imply that matching counts settle the matter.
Sofia: The status update will say the document discrepancy is being resolved. Even a corrected file would not justify telling the warehouse the goods are already released.
Chen: Exactly. Customs release must be confirmed as its own event. Our responsibility here is an accurate, authorized, traceable file, without inventing a classification or a clearance outcome.
END REFERENCE
07Supply Risk and Business ContinuityA second supplier is not yet a ready backup+

Your goal

Compare supply options while separating quoted lead time, reserved capacity, qualification, and shared disruption exposure.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

A second supplier is not yet a ready backup

Risk planner Ren and sourcing manager Julia compare supplier A, with a quoted two-week lead time, and supplier B, with a quoted four-week lead time. B reports spare capacity, but none is reserved and qualification is incomplete. Both may depend on the same coating site; this is unverified. Current usable stock equals three weeks of the stated demand rate. The team must assess continuity without declaring B immediately usable or treating two supplier names as proof of independent supply.

Ren: Risk planner / Julia: Sourcing manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the status of B's capacity?

Answer and explanation

D. Reported spare capacity, not reserved for this buyer

The supplier reports capacity but no reservation or completed qualification is supplied.

2. What does the potential shared coating site imply?

Answer and explanation

D. A possible common dependency that needs verification

A shared dependency could expose both sources to the same disruption, but the fact remains unverified.

3. What stock coverage is supplied?

Answer and explanation

C. Three weeks at the stated demand rate

The brief defines three weeks of usable stock coverage at a specified demand rate.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

B has a quoted lead time and reported capacity, not a completed qualification or a reserved production slot. A four-week quote is not necessarily the full time needed to activate the backup. The possible shared coating site remains an open fact to verify.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
single sourcing
Choosing to obtain a requirement from one supplier despite alternatives.review single-sourcing exposure
sole sourcing
Depending on the only available source for a requirement.identify sole-source constraints
dual sourcing
Using two sources for a defined supply requirement.assess a dual-sourcing strategy
source qualification
Evaluation and approval of a source for a specified requirement.complete source qualification
backup capacity
Potential supply capability intended for contingency use.verify backup capacity
capacity reservation
A defined commitment setting aside supply capability for a buyer.negotiate a capacity reservation
quoted lead time
A supplier's stated lead-time estimate or offer under specified conditions.verify the quoted lead time
activation lead time
Time needed to make a contingency operational after a trigger.estimate activation lead time
switching cost
The cost of moving a requirement to another source or arrangement.assess switching cost
tooling transfer
Moving required production tools to another site or source.plan tooling transfer
subtier supplier
A supplier further upstream than the directly contracted supplier.map subtier suppliers
common dependency
A resource or facility on which multiple supply options rely.identify a common dependency
correlated disruption
A disruption capable of affecting multiple sources together.assess correlated disruption
geographic concentration
Supply dependence clustered in the same area.reduce geographic concentration
business continuity plan
A plan for maintaining or restoring prioritized operations during disruption.test the business continuity plan
contingency trigger
A defined condition for activating an alternative action.set a contingency trigger
time to recover
The period needed to restore the specified supply or operational capability.estimate time to recover
time to survive
The period operations can continue under a defined disruption scenario.assess time to survive
recovery objective
A target for restoring a specified capability.define the recovery objective
buffer coverage
The demand period supported by a stated usable buffer.calculate buffer coverage
resilience
The ability to prepare for, respond to, and recover from disruption.strengthen supply resilience
risk register
A record of identified risks, assessments, owners, and actions.update the risk register
scenario test
An examination of a plan under specified hypothetical conditions.conduct a scenario test
continuity owner
The person accountable for a defined continuity action or plan.assign a continuity owner

Under a simplified scenario of immediate supply loss, unchanged demand, no receipts, and usable backup only after four weeks, three weeks of stock leaves a one-week gap. This illustration is conditional, not a prediction of actual qualification or recovery time.

C Notice the language

Turn backup into a list of conditions

A reassuring supplier name is not an operational continuity plan. Ask what must be qualified, reserved, transferred, or verified before the alternative can supply. This makes the resilience discussion concrete without assuming that the faster supplier is always better or the second source removes all common risks.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Compare like with like
A quotes two weeks; B quotes four, under their stated conditions.
Separate speed and resilience
A shorter quoted lead time does not answer every continuity question.
Qualify the backup
B is not yet a fully ready alternative.
Check approval
Source qualification remains incomplete.
Reserve the resource
Reported spare capacity is not capacity reserved for us.
Look upstream
Verify whether both suppliers use the same coating site.
Keep the fact open
The common-site dependency is possible, not yet confirmed.
Avoid false independence
Two supplier names do not prove independent disruption exposure.
Name the coverage basis
Current usable stock covers three weeks at the stated demand rate.
Include activation
Tooling, qualification, and allocation may affect when the backup becomes usable.
Test a bounded scenario
State demand, receipts, and activation assumptions before calculating a gap.
Separate target and estimate
A recovery objective is not proof that recovery can occur that quickly.
Define the trigger
Agree which event would activate the contingency review.
Assign the owner
Identify who will verify capacity and qualification status.
Keep action conditional
Do not promise backup supply before the required conditions are met.
Close the risk review
Record dependencies, evidence gaps, owners, and next checkpoints.
6 language and precision notes
Backup
A role in a plan, not proof of readiness.
Spare
May be available generally without being reserved for this order.
Qualified
Refers to an approved purpose and scope, not every product.
Independent
Requires attention to shared upstream resources and locations.
Coverage
Depends on usable stock and the specified demand rate.
Objective
A target to work toward, distinct from an evidenced recovery estimate.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which comparison is most useful?

Answer and explanation

A. Compare lead-time basis, qualification, reserved capacity, activation needs, and shared dependencies.

Continuity depends on operational readiness and exposure, not only the nominal lead-time comparison.

2. Stock covers three weeks. In a simplified no-receipts scenario, usable backup starts after four. Which statement is precise?

Answer and explanation

D. The conditional gap is one week; unverified activation requirements could change it.

Four minus three gives one week only under the stated scenario. A quotation, incomplete qualification, and unreserved capacity do not guarantee usable supply after four weeks.

3. Why investigate the coating site?

Answer and explanation

D. A common upstream dependency could disrupt both sources together.

Common infrastructure can make apparently separate sources vulnerable to the same event.

4. What distinguishes a recovery objective from time-to-recover evidence?

Answer and explanation

A. The objective is a target; the estimate needs support about actual restoration capability.

A desired recovery time and an evidenced estimate are different planning statements.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

A second supplier is not yet a ready backup

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ren: Risk planner / Julia: Sourcing manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • buffer coverage
  • scenario test
  • recovery objective
  • contingency trigger
  • capacity reservation
  • source qualification
  • common dependency
  • risk register
  • activation lead time
  • Correlated disruption
  1. Julia
    Supplier A quotes two weeks and supplier B quotes four. B says it has spare capacity, so the team wants to list it as our ready backup.
  2. Ren
    That overstates 1. B is not yet fully qualified for this requirement, and the capacity report does not establish an allocation to us.
  3. Julia
    I will ask whether any capacity is reserved for us. Spare capacity today is not the same as a committed slot when we need it.
  4. Ren
    Exactly. A 2 needs its own confirmation and terms. Reported availability can change before we need it, so it cannot be treated as a standing commitment.
  5. Julia
    The four-week number also may assume everything is ready to begin. If qualification or tooling work is needed first, it may not describe the whole switching period.
  6. Ren
    Include the 3. The time from deciding to switch to receiving usable supply may include steps that are not covered by the supplier's quoted lead time.
  7. Julia
    We have another unresolved question: both suppliers may use the same coating site. I do not want to repeat that as fact until it is verified.
  8. Ren
    Mark it as a possible 4. If confirmed, a disruption there could affect both suppliers despite their separate names and customer contacts.
  9. Julia
    That would change the resilience argument. Two sources could still be useful, but we should not describe their risks as independent without examining the upstream connection.
  10. Ren
    Correct. 5 is the concern. We need to understand the shared exposure rather than count suppliers and assume each protects us from every failure of the other.
  11. Julia
    Current usable inventory covers three weeks at the stated demand rate. How should that be compared with the four-week quote without creating a false recovery promise?
  12. Ren
    Use a clearly bounded 6. Assume immediate supply loss, unchanged demand, no incoming receipts, and usable backup after four weeks only for that calculation.
  13. Julia
    Under those assumptions, the stock ends after three weeks and the backup starts after four. There is a one-week gap, not complete coverage.
  14. Ren
    Yes. The 7 is shorter than the assumed wait. But actual activation could differ, especially while qualification and reservation remain incomplete.
  15. Julia
    Management wants a shorter recovery time. I will show that as a target, but what can we actually support with the evidence we have?
  16. Ren
    Exactly. A 8 expresses what we aim to restore and when. It should not be reported as an evidenced recovery estimate merely because management agreed it was desirable.
  17. Julia
    The continuity plan needs a decision point too. Otherwise we may know a backup exists on paper but not know when to begin the required review.
  18. Ren
    Define a 9 and the responsible owner. The trigger should lead to the agreed process, not authorize an unqualified source or an unsupported shipment promise.
  19. Julia
    I will revise B from ready backup to candidate pending qualification, reservation, and dependency checks. The comparison will retain A's shorter quote without treating it as the only relevant factor.
  20. Ren
    Good. Update the 10 with those conditions and checkpoints. That gives us a practical continuity discussion instead of reassurance based only on a second supplier name.

Answers and explanations
  1. source qualification Turn 2. Source qualification is incomplete for B, so its approval to supply the required item is not established.
  2. capacity reservation Turn 4. A capacity reservation commits capability to the buyer, unlike a general statement that spare capacity exists.
  3. activation lead time Turn 6. Activation lead time covers making the contingency operational, potentially beyond the supplier's quoted production lead time.
  4. common dependency Turn 8. A common dependency is a shared resource that could affect both sources, but the coating-site link remains unverified.
  5. Correlated disruption Turn 10. Correlated disruption can affect multiple sources together when they share an upstream exposure.
  6. scenario test Turn 12. A scenario test states hypothetical conditions explicitly rather than presenting an assumption-based result as a forecast.
  7. buffer coverage Turn 14. Buffer coverage measures the demand period supported by usable stock, here three weeks under the stated rate.
  8. recovery objective Turn 16. A recovery objective states a target, whereas an achievable restoration estimate requires supporting evidence.
  9. contingency trigger Turn 18. A contingency trigger identifies the condition that starts the planned alternative action or review.
  10. risk register Turn 20. The risk register records the open exposures, evidence gaps, owners, and actions needed for the continuity assessment.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Julia: Supplier A quotes two weeks and supplier B quotes four. B says it has spare capacity, so the team wants to list it as our ready backup.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Shorter lead time versus available backup capacity

Fictional continuity discussion: supplier A has a shorter lead time; supplier B has available backup capacity. Other comparative details are unconfirmed.

  1. Supply manager: Supplier A is faster. Should we exclude B from the continuity discussion?
  2. Risk planner: B has available backup capacity, so lead time alone doesn't describe the business-continuity tradeoff.
  3. Supply manager: Which common criteria should we use without pretending we already know the full comparison?
  4. Risk planner: Lead time, usable capacity, qualification status, and relevant supply constraints, with unknown entries marked clearly.
  5. Supply manager: Can we call B fully interchangeable just because its capacity is available?
  6. Risk planner: No. Capacity doesn't establish that its supply meets our requirements or is authorized for use.
  7. Supply manager: Then dual sourcing is an option to assess, not a completed resilience measure.
  8. Risk planner: Exactly. Compare the evidence and dependencies before promising that backup supply eliminates disruption risk.
  9. Supply manager: I'll present A's shorter lead time and B's capacity alongside the missing checks.
  10. Risk planner: I'll organize the like-for-like assessment so the decision maker sees both the known advantage and the unresolved conditions.
Two suppliers with one shared transport constraint

Separate fictional continuity case: suppliers C and D are separate companies but both proposed routes use the same port; alternate routing has not been assessed.

  1. Procurement director: We have two suppliers. Can the continuity slide say the route risk is diversified?
  2. Logistics risk analyst: Both proposed routes use the same port, so that particular constraint is still shared.
  3. Procurement director: Does that mean having two suppliers provides no continuity benefit at all?
  4. Logistics risk analyst: Not necessarily. Supplier dependence and transport-route dependence are different aspects to compare.
  5. Procurement director: What should our options table show so neither benefit nor limitation is hidden?
  6. Logistics risk analyst: Supplier capacity, route dependencies, lead times, and any verified alternatives under the same assumptions.
  7. Procurement director: We haven't assessed another port, so I shouldn't list rerouting as immediately available.
  8. Logistics risk analyst: Correct. Treat it as an assessment option with unconfirmed feasibility and timing.
  9. Procurement director: I'll qualify the dual-sourcing statement and request the route comparison before presenting the plan as complete.
  10. Logistics risk analyst: I'll show the shared constraint explicitly and avoid suggesting that two company names remove every common exposure.
A backup allocation smaller than the required volume

Separate fictional planning case: the continuity requirement is 1,000 units; backup supplier E offers a confirmed allocation of 300. Additional capacity is unknown.

  1. Continuity planner: Supplier E has confirmed three hundred units against our thousand-unit requirement.
  2. Operations director: Can we describe that supplier as a complete backup for the month?
  3. Continuity planner: No. The confirmed allocation covers three hundred, leaving seven hundred units without a confirmed backup here.
  4. Operations director: Would it be fair to dismiss the option because it doesn't cover the entire requirement?
  5. Continuity planner: Not necessarily. It offers partial coverage; compare that benefit with cost, timing, and other unresolved constraints.
  6. Operations director: Should we assume the supplier can expand to a thousand if the disruption actually happens?
  7. Continuity planner: No. Additional capacity is unknown and needs confirmation before it enters the committed plan.
  8. Operations director: I'll describe the partial allocation accurately and ask what other reviewed options could cover the gap.
  9. Continuity planner: I'll compare those options against the same thousand-unit requirement rather than change the denominator.
  10. Operations director: Good. Our business-continuity discussion should show both confirmed coverage and the exposure that remains.

Usable stock covers two weeks at unchanged demand. In a simplified scenario with no incoming receipts, usable backup begins after five weeks. Capacity is not reserved.

Answer and explanation

Answer and explanation

C. unchanged

3. Planner: "Backup capacity still lacks a ___."

Answer and explanation

D. reservation

Reported capability is not the same as supply capacity committed to this buyer.

Answer and explanation

The result depends on stated assumptions and does not promise actual recovery timing.

Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): A quotes two weeks; B quotes four, under their stated conditions. / A shorter quoted lead time does not answer every continuity question. / B is not yet a fully ready alternative. / Source qualification remains incomplete. / Reported spare capacity is not capacity reserved for us. / Verify whether both suppliers use the same coating site. / The common-site dependency is possible, not yet confirmed. / Two supplier names do not prove independent disruption exposure. / Current usable stock covers three weeks at the stated demand rate. / Tooling, qualification, and allocation may affect when the backup becomes usable. / State demand, receipts, and activation assumptions before calculating a gap. / A recovery objective is not proof that recovery can occur that quickly. / Agree which event would activate the contingency review. / Identify who will verify capacity and qualification status. / Do not promise backup supply before the required conditions are met. / Record dependencies, evidence gaps, owners, and next checkpoints.
Vocabulary: single sourcing: Choosing to obtain a requirement from one supplier despite alternatives. / sole sourcing: Depending on the only available source for a requirement. / dual sourcing: Using two sources for a defined supply requirement. / source qualification: Evaluation and approval of a source for a specified requirement. / backup capacity: Potential supply capability intended for contingency use. / capacity reservation: A defined commitment setting aside supply capability for a buyer. / quoted lead time: A supplier's stated lead-time estimate or offer under specified conditions. / activation lead time: Time needed to make a contingency operational after a trigger. / switching cost: The cost of moving a requirement to another source or arrangement. / tooling transfer: Moving required production tools to another site or source. / subtier supplier: A supplier further upstream than the directly contracted supplier. / common dependency: A resource or facility on which multiple supply options rely. / correlated disruption: A disruption capable of affecting multiple sources together. / geographic concentration: Supply dependence clustered in the same area. / business continuity plan: A plan for maintaining or restoring prioritized operations during disruption. / contingency trigger: A defined condition for activating an alternative action. / time to recover: The period needed to restore the specified supply or operational capability. / time to survive: The period operations can continue under a defined disruption scenario. / recovery objective: A target for restoring a specified capability. / buffer coverage: The demand period supported by a stated usable buffer. / resilience: The ability to prepare for, respond to, and recover from disruption. / risk register: A record of identified risks, assessments, owners, and actions. / scenario test: An examination of a plan under specified hypothetical conditions. / continuity owner: The person accountable for a defined continuity action or plan.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, distinguishing reported spare capacity from a reservation and a possible common site from a verified one. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep the one-week gap conditional on the printed simplified scenario. Complete and check the transfer. Read five weeks minus two as three, then state the unchanged-demand and unreserved-capacity conditions.
Partner: Ren: Risk planner / Julia: Sourcing manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Usable stock covers two weeks at unchanged demand. In a simplified scenario with no incoming receipts, usable backup begins after five weeks. Capacity is not reserved.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Julia: The continuity plan needs a decision point too. Otherwise we may know a backup exists on paper but not know when to begin the required review.
Ren: Define a contingency trigger and the responsible owner. The trigger should lead to the agreed process, not authorize an unqualified source or an unsupported shipment promise.
Julia: I will revise B from ready backup to candidate pending qualification, reservation, and dependency checks. The comparison will retain A's shorter quote without treating it as the only relevant factor.
Ren: Good. Update the risk register with those conditions and checkpoints. That gives us a practical continuity discussion instead of reassurance based only on a second supplier name.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Course: Supply Chain and Logistics English
Professional audience: supply-chain managers, planners, buyers, logistics coordinators, warehouse leaders, procurement teams, customs staff, and operations managers
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not customs, legal, financial, transport-safety, or inventory-policy advice. All figures, shipments, organizations, and negotiations are invented. Use applicable law, governing contracts, current trade rules, authorized decisions, and qualified review. US customs examples are identified; requirements differ by jurisdiction.
