Dialogue 05: A customs document mismatch
Original fictional setting: A shipment is awaiting clarification at the destination port.
Professional roles: Customs broker / Logistics coordinator
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Customs broker: The commercial invoice and packing list show different quantities.
Logistics coordinator: The invoice lists 100 cartons; the packing list lists 96. I'll check the loading record.
Customs broker: Please obtain corrected documents from the responsible shipper. Don't just edit the figures here.
Logistics coordinator: Does the tariff classification need review too?
Customs broker: The product description is incomplete, so classification needs confirmation from the appropriate specialist.
Logistics coordinator: I'll request the detailed specification and reconcile the carton count.
Customs broker: Once those are available, we can assess the entry requirements.
Logistics coordinator: I'll tell the customer clearance remains pending and avoid promising a delivery date.
Vocabulary in this exchange: commercial invoice: A trade document describing the goods sold and relevant transaction details.
END REFERENCE

Course: Supply Chain and Logistics English

Dialogue 06: Carrier performance review
Original fictional setting: Procurement challenges a carrier's on-time delivery report.
Professional roles: Procurement manager / Carrier account manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Procurement manager: Your scorecard says 96 percent on time. Ours says 89 percent.
Carrier account manager: We measure arrival within the booked delivery day. What window are you using?
Procurement manager: The appointment slot, including rejected deliveries. Are those in your denominator?
Carrier account manager: No. We excluded failed delivery attempts.
Procurement manager: That explains part of the gap. Let's reconcile shipment IDs before discussing remedies.
Carrier account manager: I'll send the event-level records and exclusion rules.
Procurement manager: We'll agree on one definition for next month's review.
Carrier account manager: And I'll separately investigate the repeated misses at the northern distribution center.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 07: Supplier recovery plan
Original fictional setting: A supplier has missed two weekly commitments.
Professional roles: Supplier development lead / Supplier production manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Supplier development lead: We need a recovery plan tied to actual capacity. What is constraining output?
Supplier production manager: Final test is the bottleneck. The second fixture is still being qualified.
Supplier development lead: What can the current fixture deliver reliably?
Supplier production manager: About 200 units a day on the present shift pattern.
Supplier development lead: Show that as the base plan. Keep the second fixture as a conditional improvement.
Supplier production manager: I'll include daily milestones and the qualification decision date.
Supplier development lead: Please also identify the owner for each action.
Supplier production manager: I'll send the plan today and report shipment quantities against it each morning.
Vocabulary in this exchange: owner: Named person or role accountable for a decision, action, deliverable, or risk.
END REFERENCE

Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Dialogue 07: Supplier recovery plan
Original fictional setting: A supplier has missed two weekly commitments.
Professional roles: Supplier development lead / Supplier production manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Supplier development lead: We need a recovery plan tied to actual capacity. What is constraining output?
Supplier production manager: Final test is the bottleneck. The second fixture is still being qualified.
Supplier development lead: What can the current fixture deliver reliably?
Supplier production manager: About 200 units a day on the present shift pattern.
Supplier development lead: Show that as the base plan. Keep the second fixture as a conditional improvement.
Supplier production manager: I'll include daily milestones and the qualification decision date.
Supplier development lead: Please also identify the owner for each action.
Supplier production manager: I'll send the plan today and report shipment quantities against it each morning.
Vocabulary in this exchange: owner: Named person or role accountable for a decision, action, deliverable, or risk.
END REFERENCE
Dialogue 08: Disruption handoff
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Dialogue 08: Disruption handoff
Original fictional setting: A storm has interrupted a planned delivery route.
Professional roles: Logistics coordinator / Customer service lead
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Logistics coordinator: The linehaul is delayed, and the carrier hasn't confirmed a revised arrival.
Customer service lead: Do we know which customer orders are on the trailer?
Logistics coordinator: Yes. I've attached the manifest and the last verified tracking event.
Customer service lead: Can we offer delivery tomorrow?
Logistics coordinator: Not yet. The alternate route is still being assessed.
Customer service lead: I'll explain the delay and give customers a time for the next update.
Logistics coordinator: I'll own the carrier follow-up and flag any confirmed change immediately.
Customer service lead: I'll record individual customer constraints so we can prioritize when capacity returns.
Vocabulary in this exchange: carrier: A company that transports goods or people; in insurance, an insurer.
END REFERENCE
Create more scenario dialogues — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

REFERENCE
Course: Supply Chain and Logistics English
Professional audience: supply-chain managers, planners, buyers, logistics coordinators, warehouse leaders, procurement teams, customs staff, and operations managers
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not customs, legal, financial, transport-safety, or inventory-policy advice. All figures, shipments, organizations, and negotiations are invented. Use applicable law, governing contracts, current trade rules, authorized decisions, and qualified review. US customs examples are identified; requirements differ by jurisdiction.
Lesson 01: Demand Planning and Forecast Bias
Original fictional case: Demand planner Aisha and sales lead Martin review monthly forecasts of 120, 130, and 140 units against verified demand of 100, 110, and 120. For this case, signed error means forecast minus actual demand. Next month's baseline is 120 units. Sales proposes a 30-unit promotional uplift but has no order evidence for it. Aisha and Martin must describe the three-month pattern and preserve the promotion as a scenario without treating either historical bias or future uplift as a certainty forever.
Language workshop: Three optimistic forecasts and a new promotion
Communication goal: Explain signed forecast error, distinguish bias from error size, and keep an unconfirmed promotional uplift separate from the baseline.
Language guidance: With error defined as forecast minus actual, the three errors are +20, +20, and +20 units. Total error is +60 and mean error is +20. Positive means overforecasting under this convention; other systems may reverse the sign.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Here we define signed error as forecast minus actual demand. / Each of the three forecasts exceeded actual demand by 20 units. / Mean signed error is plus 20 units over these three months. / This pattern does not prove every future forecast will be high. / Absolute error measures the miss without its sign. / The next-month baseline remains 120 units. / Sales proposes a 30-unit promotional uplift. / That produces a 150-unit scenario, not confirmed demand. / What order or response evidence supports the proposed increment? / A future promotion does not change the earlier forecast errors. / Compare the forecast version that existed at the agreed planning point. / A sales target is not automatically an unbiased demand estimate. / Is the uplift additional demand or partly shifted from another item? / Keep the adjustment, owner, and rationale visible. / Assess whether the adjustment improves the defined forecast measure. / Present baseline, upside, and supporting evidence separately.
Vocabulary: demand forecast: An estimate of future demand for a defined item and period. / actual demand: The observed demand under the stated measurement definition. / forecast bias: A tendency for forecasts to be systematically too high or too low. / signed error: The forecast difference calculated with an explicitly stated direction. / mean error: The average signed forecast error across the defined observations. / absolute error: The size of a forecast error without its positive or negative sign. / mean absolute error: The average absolute error across the defined observations. / overforecast: A forecast greater than the corresponding actual demand. / underforecast: A forecast below the corresponding actual demand. / baseline forecast: The reference forecast before a separately identified adjustment. / promotional uplift: Additional demand expected because of a promotion. / incremental demand: Demand additional to the specified base rather than displaced from elsewhere. / cannibalization: A gain in one product's demand at the expense of another's. / seasonality: A recurring demand pattern tied to a calendar or cycle. / trend: A sustained directional movement in a series over time. / forecast horizon: The future period covered by a forecast. / forecast lag: The time between making a forecast and the period being forecast. / forecast snapshot: A saved version of the forecast at a specified time. / consensus forecast: A forecast agreed through the relevant cross-functional planning process. / demand signal: Information indicating demand behavior or potential change. / booked order: An order recorded under the organization's order definition. / scenario assumption: A premise used to construct a possible future case. / forecast override: A deliberate adjustment to a model or baseline forecast. / forecast value added: Improvement in a defined forecast measure from a specified planning step.
Speaking task: Read the corrected forecast dialogue in pairs. Stress plus twenty, mean, and absolute when comparing the historical errors. Switch roles. Read turns 9-20, distinguishing the 120-unit baseline, proposed 30-unit uplift, and 150-unit scenario. Complete and check the transfer. Read 80 minus 100 as minus 20, and keep the 105-unit next-month scenario separate from orders.
Partner: Aisha: Demand planner / Martin: Sales lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A forecast is 80 units and actual demand is 100. Error is defined as forecast minus actual. Next month has a 90-unit baseline and a proposed 15-unit uplift without orders.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Martin: When the next results arrive, let's evaluate the forecast version we actually used. Revising old numbers after seeing demand would make the performance comparison misleading.
Aisha: Preserve the forecast snapshot and its planning date. We need a consistent lag and definition if we want to learn whether the adjustment helped.
Martin: I will present one hundred twenty as baseline and one hundred fifty as the promotion scenario, with the evidence gap stated. The historical errors will remain unchanged.
Aisha: Good. Record the proposed forecast override with its owner and rationale. That gives the opportunity a fair assessment without disguising uncertainty or treating the scenario as a customer commitment.
END REFERENCE

For teachers and study partners

Ask learners to respond before revealing the model. Give feedback on one meaning issue and one language pattern, then let them repeat. For mixed levels, offer the frames first and remove them in the second round.
